Redcare · RDC-DE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Redcare

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 53,267 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment strongly supports Redcare's core engine. Demand for online prescription fulfilment in Germany is shifting structurally and accelerating. Regulation has, within 15 months, legalised bonuses (BGH), raised the per-pack fee twice, lengthened repeat cycles and removed the CardLink cliff to March 2028. The duopoly with DocMorris is, for now, rational.

The three things the Consumer must not miss:

  1. Profit comes per pack and is set by the state. That gives high predictability and a concentrated political tail risk.
  2. OTC is the cyclical, competitive half. It caused the late-2025 slowdown and the March 2026 reset, and it remains the main source of forecast error.
  3. The market regime is hostile to the stock's profile even as the fundamentals improve.

Net: the strongest external tailwind set among European healthcare retail names, carrying a state-granted fragility.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0255C-0558C-0755C-0966C-3570C-3962
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

B553rd of 11

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Intensity fell in H1 2026 from the promotional peak of late 2024 and late 2025. It is now turning modestly up in Rx from a benign base, and staying high but stable in OTC.

Evidence that it fell:

  • Both leaders cut marketing: Redcare to 5.1% of revenue in Q2 2026 (-210bp year on year), and DocMorris by CHF 14m, a 310bp improvement in its marketing ratio.
  • Experts describe both as "shifting focus from growth to profitability" with a "constructive relationship" aimed at growing the online market.
  • Sub-scale players are exiting: Disapo and Sanicare both went insolvent within two months.
  • dm's online OTC launch (about 2,500 products) had no measurable impact.

Evidence that it is turning up over the next 6 to 12 months:

  1. DocMorris has refinanced (a CHF 200m capital raise), reached adjusted EBITDA break-even in Q3 2026 ahead of schedule, and raised its guidance, with German Rx up 78% against Redcare's 56%. Morgan Stanley flags that it could redeploy capital into promotional Rx campaigns.
  2. The co-payment rise in January 2027 (minimum €5 to €7.50, maximum €10 to €15) creates a natural battleground. Whoever covers more of the patient's co-payment wins chronic patients. DocMorris management declined to commit to covering the full increase, which suggests restraint for now.
  3. Rossmann plans a Dutch-based OTC and Rx launch in late 2026. Deutsche Bank calls it "not a major threat", but it adds noise to OTC pricing.
  4. gesund.de, the wholesaler-backed platform for local pharmacies, has CardLink transfers to about 5,000 pharmacies and a PAYBACK tie-up reaching 31m users.

In OTC, price transparency through comparison sites (Medizinfuchs) keeps intensity structurally high. Redcare prices up to 10% below competitors on key items.

Who starts a price war matters. Redcare itself drove the late-2024 and late-2025 promotional peaks: the Q4 2025 adjusted EBITDA margin fell to 1.6% on bonus campaigns. The risk is therefore as much Redcare's own choice under a new, growth-minded marketplace chief executive as DocMorris'.

Skew: mildly negative over 12 months. Cone: wide, because a bonus war in 2027 could cost tens of basis points of margin.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

A581st of 11

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: The net effect is slightly positive over 18 months, as scale and automation offsetting wage and carrier inflation. The one under-watched risk is wholesaler terms.

  • Product cost (about 78% of revenue).
  • German Rx carries no inflation risk. Pharmacy margin is regulated as a fixed fee plus a percentage, so drug-price inflation passes straight through.
  • In OTC and beauty, Redcare's procurement spend was €2.44bn in FY25 (€1.92bn in FY24). About 70% is bought direct from manufacturers (Bayer, Sanofi, P&G, L'Oréal) and 30% through three or four wholesalers. Volume discounts reach up to 30% on non-Rx inventory.
  • Statutory wholesale surcharge rules (up to 3.15%, capped at €37.80, plus a non-discountable €0.73 per pack) cap what wholesalers can concede on Rx. Redcare's Rx buying terms are therefore structurally bounded, for it and for rivals alike.
  • Supplier concentration. The German wholesale tier is an oligopoly (Phoenix, Alliance/GEHE, Sanacorp). Management acknowledged "ongoing discussions with major pharmaceutical wholesale partners regarding changing purchase conditions". This is undisclosed in size and is the input-side item to watch. Some manufacturers resist direct deals with cross-border operators for fear of price erosion (the Czech launch delays). Wholesalers are building their own defence through gesund.de.
  • Labour. The Venlo area labour market is tight. Warehouse turnover was 39.1% in 2025 (34.9% in 2024), bottom quartile against a 25-30% sector base. Wage inflation is real. The offsets are structural:
  • Sevenum automation: a €100m programme, commissioning in Q1 2027, 70% lower labour cost per order and about 50bp of EBITDA per Deutsche Bank.
  • Pilsen (Blatnice): live since late 2025, with 15m orders a year of non-Rx capacity at Czech wages. Czech law forbids shipping Rx across the border, so Pilsen serves Austria and OTC only.
  • Carriers. DHL and others at about €3 per package. The signature requirement adds about €0.19 per Rx delivery.
  • Supplier credit. Trade payables of €252m (33 days) fund the model and give a structural working-capital advantage. They are also a dependency.
  • Currency. 99% of inventory is bought in reporting currencies. Swiss franc costs are naturally hedged inside MediService.

Peer contrast. Georg Fischer and CVS illustrate input-price exposure that Redcare does not have in Rx, because regulation passes it through.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

B+552nd of 11

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Macro bites hardest at the level of the share price's discount rate and risk appetite, not at the level of Redcare's fundamentals.

At the company level, macro is moderate. About 40% of revenue (German and Swiss Rx) is reimbursed by statutory insurance and is macro-insensitive (DNB Carnegie, Jefferies). The roughly 60% non-Rx is discretionary-tinged:

  • euro-area inflation was 3.2% in May and 2.8% in June 2026, with a flash reading of 3.76% in September;
  • consumer confidence was -17.9 in October 2026;
  • beauty trading-down is under way: about 24% of beauty consumers traded down (KT&Partners);
  • French consumer spending fell 0.5% month on month in August 2026.

Higher co-payments in 2027 are a macro-policy squeeze on patients that, perversely, helps the online Rx channel.

Currency is minor. A 10% franc move changes revenue by €56.6m but EBITDA by only €1.7m and EPS by €0.06.

At the sector level (B.8), the Fitness for Fighting digest is Neutral. The cohort's median stock is -2.2% over 12 months; the pharmacies are +36.4% over six months.

At the market level, the regime is risk-off and broadening. The US 10-year is near 5.10%, Brent is at $111, French spreads have spread into Italy, and defensives are beating cyclicals on all five windows. The Momentum tilt moved to Underweight and the Fragility tilt is a Double Underweight. Redcare is a loss-making, high-beta, convertible-financed mid-cap with 17% short interest (partly convertible hedging), up 62.6% in six months. That is exactly the profile the regime punishes on multiple, not on earnings.

Interest rates matter twice. First, through the discount rate on a long-duration cash flow that turns positive only in FY27. Second, but minimally, through the coupon: the convertible pays 1.75% fixed to 2032.

Peer reference. Galenica, the defensive omnichannel name, is the only cohort stock up over the year (+1.4%).

Implication. The fundamentals are macro-resilient. The multiple is macro-exposed. In a deeper risk-off the shares could de-rate even on upgrades; this is covered under C-82 and C-83.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–66

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Six further dynamics are material. On balance they are mildly positive.

Positive:

  1. Structural decline of local pharmacies. Count below 16,600, staff shortages, falling real reimbursement. Each closure pushes chronic patients to choose a new provider, which is a "moment of truth" that online can win. Retail EBIT margins of local pharmacies are below 3% (Inderes), so they cannot match bonuses.
  2. Medicine shortages and EU cross-border e-Rx. The European Health Data Space targets EU-wide cross-border e-prescriptions by 2029. Redcare commissioned a shortage study (October 2026) and is positioning as a redistribution platform. This is a long-dated option.
  3. Swiss liberalisation of mail-order. Projected for no earlier than 2029-30 (Deutsche Bank). Through the Galenica joint venture Redcare is the natural winner, and Galenica sees it as an opportunity rather than a threat.
  4. Demographics and therapy mix. Ageing increases chronic volumes. Specialty and GLP-1 therapies raise basket values: Rx basket +14% year on year, average order value €68.37 in Q2 2026.

Negative:

  1. Swiss pricing pressure. Federal Office of Public Health price cuts and generic substitution compress MediService revenue per script. The segment is low-margin: a 5% franc move shifts EBITDA by only €0.85m on €28.3m of revenue, implying about 3%.
  2. Political economy of success. The more share online pharmacies take, the more pressure local pharmacists exert. Minister Linnemann already criticised bonuses. This is a balancing loop that grows with Redcare's success; see C-37 and the Consumer's later feedback-loop question.

One market-structure dynamic: index and positioning flows. Redcare was excluded from the STOXX Europe 600 in September 2025. It has 17% short interest, partly convertible arbitrage hedging. Recovery in market capitalisation (€1.6bn now) raises the chance of re-inclusion and of short covering.

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

A701st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The threat is low overall. It is concentrated in OTC, where adjacent drugstore entrants can take a slice. Rx is protected.

  1. Adjacent entrants (drugstore chains dm and Rossmann; quick-commerce platforms such as Wolt and Uber Direct). Moderate threat to OTC, low to Rx. - dm launched online OTC with about 2,500 products: "no operational impact"; an inferior interface. Experts see a long-run 10-15% share of online OTC, at most. - Rossmann plans a Dutch-based OTC and Rx pharmacy in late 2026. Cold-chain and gematik requirements slow it, and Deutsche Bank's survey shows low willingness, especially among patients over 55, to buy Rx from drugstores. - Quick commerce is a pilot in Sweden (Wolt with DOZ Apotek) and threatens acute and OTC convenience only.
  2. Standard entrants (new mail-order pharmacies): negligible. Minimum efficient scale is rising and Disapo and Sanicare failed.
  3. Orthogonal or innovative entrants: low now, the most important to monitor long term. - Amazon has made a €60bn European investment, but has a ten-year failure in EU Rx, low incentive under fixed prices and very low consumer trust for Rx (score 3.7). - Telemedicine-led pharmacies: TeleClinic, and oral GLP-1 telehealth funnels. - Insurer-led apps, if digital-referral rules loosen. - Manufacturer direct-to-consumer: minimal in Europe.

The real "new entrant" risk is regulatory deregulation, for example allowing marketplaces to sell Rx or insurers to steer prescriptions. That is not in prospect.

Skew for 18 months: Rossmann's launch is a headline risk for the shares (as dm was in December 2024) more than a fundamental one.

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

A621st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Mostly a support. Suppliers fund the model through 33 days of trade credit, accept direct sourcing and buy retail media. There is one live risk, the renegotiation of wholesale purchase conditions, and one structural cap, the statutory wholesale pricing rules.

Support:

  • Direct manufacturer agreements (Bayer, Sanofi, P&G Health, L'Oréal) for about 70% of non-Rx procurement.
  • Volume discounts up to 30% on non-Rx.
  • More than 150 pharmaceutical suppliers buying retail media.
  • Exclusive pharmacy-channel brands (high-end cosmetics sold only through pharmacies).
  • Payables of €252.2m against inventories of €213.3m.
  • Supplier Code of Conduct covers 75.6% of spend.

Risks:

  1. Wholesaler purchase conditions are under discussion (management, Leadership digest). The size and timing are undisclosed. Wholesalers are losing value to online channels and may claw back terms. A 0.25-point change in Rx purchase terms on about €1.4bn of Rx revenue (Germany and Switzerland) would be about €3-4m. That is material against a 3% EBITDA margin. This is an illustrative sensitivity, not a forecast.
  2. Statutory caps on wholesale surcharges and the fixed €0.73 per pack mean Rx procurement advantages are bounded. Scale cannot buy much more on Rx.
  3. Manufacturers' fear of cross-border price erosion limits direct deals in some markets (the Czech launch).
  4. Shortage allocation. Wholesalers prioritise their largest pharmacy customers in shortages. Redcare is large, so it is probably favoured, but the point is unverified.
  5. Carriers. Last-mile price rises and the signature rule.

Peer reference. Apotea maintained a 27.5% gross margin by improving OTC procurement terms as Rx mix rose. That is the template Redcare needs on OTC.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0178C-0372C-0478C-0882C-3272C-3770C-4382
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

A781st of 11

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Two demands with opposite characters sit inside Redcare, and the Consumer must model them separately.

Prescription demand is changing structurally, and accelerating. The German statutory Rx market is about €55bn and grows about 4.2% a year (Jefferies) on ageing. What is changing is the channel, not total demand. E-prescriptions became mandatory in January 2024 and now cover 89-97% of statutory scripts: 52.3m redeemed in March 2026, 48.3m in September 2026, with 94% of pharmacies connected. Yet only about 1-2% of Rx value is filled online.

The last six months accelerated this shift against the prior 12 to 18 months:

  • Online medicine sales in Germany rose 9.8% in Q1 2026 and 13.9% in Q2 2026 (BEVH), the fastest since the mandate.
  • Redcare's German Rx grew 55%, 58% and 56% in Q1, Q2 and Q3 2026.
  • DocMorris' German Rx growth accelerated to 78% in Q3 2026.

Three structural changes started on or after 1 July 2026 and add to the pull:

  • Six-month repeat prescriptions for stable chronic patients, which remove a doctor visit per cycle.
  • A 50% rise in patient co-payments in 2027, which raises the value of the bonuses that cross-border mail-order pharmacies are legally allowed to pay.
  • The Fixum increases.

Visibility is genuinely two or more years. CardLink, the app-based redemption route, is licensed to 31 March 2028. The fee changes are enacted law. The successor, Proof of Patient Presence (PoPP), starts its Phase 2 in Q4 2027.

Non-prescription demand (OTC, beauty, supplements, about 60% of revenue) is cyclical, mature and soft.

  • German OTC is already about 25-26% online (expert), so channel shift is largely spent.
  • Consumer confidence in the euro area was -17.9 in October 2026.
  • European beauty growth slowed to about 3%, and consumers are trading down.
  • The July 2026 heatwave pushed German non-Rx growth to single digits.

DACH non-Rx grew 9% in H1 2026 (Q1 9%, Q2 13%) against guidance of 10-12%. Visibility here is quarters, not years, and it is weather- and promotion-sensitive.

Dimensions.

  • Skew: positive for Rx and neutral-to-negative for OTC over the next 12 months.
  • Cone of outcomes: narrow for Rx volume direction, because adoption is a stock that builds, while the slope is uncertain (low-to-mid single-digit online penetration by FY28 in the Jefferies base case, against a 10% medium-term ceiling from a former Shop Apotheke chief executive). The cone is wide for OTC.
  • Peer check: Sweden reached 27% online penetration of the total pharmacy market and about 12% of Rx. Apotea's chief executive notes that Rx switches online more slowly than beauty, which argues for a gradual German slope rather than a cliff.

Implication for the model: the group's growth mix is shifting towards a structural, regulated, inelastic driver. That raises the predictability of gross profit even as reported revenue growth decelerates.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

A721st of 11

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: The dominant disruption force, digitisation of the prescription, is strengthening and acts for Redcare, which is the disruptor, not the disrupted. Two secondary forces cut the other way: telemedicine-integrated competitors, and a technology transition in 2027-28.

  1. E-prescription and app redemption (strengthening, positive). E-Rx removed the local pharmacy's main structural advantage, which was proximity to the doctor and physical receipt of the paper script. Gematik's PoPP draft specification of 7 September 2026 adds Health ID and biometric authentication and removes the SMS step, which lowers onboarding friction further. Brick-and-mortar real-estate value is fading (ForeignAffairs.co.nz). Pharmacy numbers fell below 16,600 in 2026.
  2. Big-tech entry (being disproved). Amazon has failed to grow Rx share in Europe over ten years. Pharmacist staffing, licensed storage and 27 national systems block it. Deutsche Bank's survey gave Amazon a willingness-to-order score of only 3.7. Morgan Stanley sees low incentive because German Rx prices are fixed.
  3. Telemedicine (strengthening, mixed). DocMorris' TeleClinic grew 48% in H1 2026 to €17.6m, carries an EBITDA margin in the low 30s, and contributes 11% of DocMorris' gross profit. It routes prescriptions directly into DocMorris' pharmacy. Redcare has no comparable owned funnel, and is in litigation over Zava teleprescriptions. This is the one disruption vector where the smaller rival holds the better position. Oral GLP-1 (Wegovy) prescribing through telehealth enlarges that funnel.
  4. Quick commerce and same-day local delivery (weak, slightly negative). Wolt pilots and gesund.de exist; Redcare counters with its "NOW!" same-day partnerships. This matters for acute demand, which online pharmacies largely concede anyway.
  5. Artificial intelligence (operating, not a moat). Redcare's AI pricing tools added about 2 percentage points to German non-Rx growth. DocMorris' "AI-First" programme targets CHF 15m of annual savings and 100 fewer full-time staff. Both use it, so it narrows cost gaps rather than widening them.
  6. Direct-to-consumer by manufacturers (disproved for Europe). Morgan Stanley puts the cash-pay US platforms at about 3% of branded spend.

Transition risk: CardLink runs to March 2028; PoPP Phase 2 starts in Q4 2027. If elderly chronic patients struggle with biometric Health ID, redemptions could dip. Gematik has kept the option of extending CardLink further, which caps the downside.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

A781st of 11

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Regulatory change is large, mostly enacted and mostly favourable for 2026-27. It is the single biggest exogenous determinant of the company's profit, and it is also the main source of fragility.

Favourable and enacted:

  • Fixum. €8.35 to €9.00 (1 July 2026) to €9.50 (1 January 2027).
  • CardLink extended to 31 March 2028, removing the January 2027 cliff.
  • Six-month repeat prescriptions from 1 July 2026.
  • Planned stricter temperature controls on mail-order shipments abandoned. These were feared to add material cost.
  • Federal Court of Justice (BGH) rulings. In July 2025 the court confirmed that EU mail-order pharmacies are not bound by German fixed-price rules, so they may pay Rx bonuses. In November 2025 it found the pharmacists' chamber potentially liable for damages for blocking DocMorris' advertising.
  • Co-payment rise in 2027 (+50%). This raises the value of the bonus proposition, which only foreign mail-order pharmacies can legally offer.
  • Health Minister Carsten Linnemann refused to split online and offline policy, though he criticised bonuses.

Unfavourable or in force:

  • Statutory rebate to insurers rises from €1.77 to €2.07 per pack in January 2027: €2-3m a year net per Deutsche Bank, or €5.3m gross in its Scenario 1.
  • Signature-on-delivery costs about €0.19 per delivery, below €1m a year.
  • Karlsruhe Higher Regional Court (9 December 2025) ruled that €25 e-Rx vouchers redeemable against OTC breach advertising law, which limits how bonuses are presented.
  • Pharmacists' associations demanded a mail-order Rx ban at the September 2026 Deutscher Apothekertag. The motion is non-binding, and the Bundestag's research service has significant doubts about compatibility with EU law.
  • The Health Finance Commission proposed 66 measures to save €42.3bn in 2027, including higher cost-sharing and dynamic rebates. This is a standing risk of further rebate increases.

Net effect, quantified by Deutsche Bank (3 June 2026). The net adjusted EBITDA tailwind for Redcare in 2027 is €9.5m (at €70 per pack, 12.5m packs) to €13.3m (at €50 per pack, 17.6m packs), or 7.7-10.8% of consensus 2027 EBITDA as it then stood. It rises to €12.3-17.2m in 2028 and €20.8-29.1m by 2030. DocMorris' benefit is only €3.7-5.2m in 2027, so the reform widens Redcare's absolute profit lead.

How it bears. The regime makes Rx gross profit a quasi-utility income per pack, indexed by political decision rather than by competition. The fragility is that what the state grants it can trim. Every €0.10 of rebate per pack is worth roughly €1.3-1.8m a year at 2027 pack volumes. A bonus ban (as in 2021) or a mail-order ban (judged very unlikely under EU law) would be value-destroying.

Skew: positive for 2026-27. Cone: the left tail is small in probability and large in magnitude.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

A821st of 11

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Yes, strongly and measurably. The industry's key driver, the migration of German statutory Rx from local pharmacies to online after the e-Rx mandate, is accelerating, and Redcare is its largest beneficiary.

Evidence of acceleration in the last six months:

  • Redcare's German Rx grew 55% (Q1), 58% (Q2) and 56% (Q3 2026); September was +51% against a tougher base.
  • Guidance was raised from more than €670m (March) to €680-720m (June) to €730-760m (September), that is from +35-43% to +45-51%.
  • DocMorris Rx went from +46% in Q2 to +78% in Q3 2026.
  • BEVH online medicine sales went from +9.8% in Q1 to +13.9% in Q2 2026.
  • Redcare's active app users rose 7% year on year in September 2026, while DocMorris' fell 15%.

Mechanisms layering on:

  1. Six-month repeat cycles from July 2026.
  2. CardLink extended to March 2028, then PoPP with Health ID.
  3. The 2027 co-payment rise, which bonuses can offset.
  4. Pharmacy closures (below 16,600).
  5. Chronic cohort compounding. The 2025 German Rx cohorts produced about four times the revenue and twice the accumulated gross profit of non-Rx cohorts after six quarters. Repeat orders are 90% of orders.

Caution: the driver is accelerating in value, less in patient numbers. Active German Rx customers rose only about 4% quarter on quarter in Q2 2026. Awareness of CardLink was only 35% in Deutsche Bank's survey, and about 80% of Germans are unaware of digital redemption. That is both the risk (acquisition is getting harder) and the runway (the unaware majority remains to be converted).

Peer reference. Sweden reached 27% online penetration with a centralised e-Rx database. German density (17 km² per pharmacy against Sweden's 285 km²) argues for a slower German curve.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

A721st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Demand from existing Rx customers is strong and sticky, and supports the thesis well. Acquisition of new Rx patients is slowing, and that is where the fragility lies.

Supportive:

  • Repeat orders are 90% of orders (stable Q1 to Q2 2026; 88% in 2024).
  • NPS is 74 overall; e-Rx NPS is 77 in Q2 and 80 in August 2026, the highest since launch.
  • Rx basket is +14% year on year (specialty and chronic mix); the average order value rose to €68.37.
  • The mixed-order rate is 40.8%.
  • The 2025 Rx cohorts produced about 4x the revenue and 2x the gross profit of non-Rx cohorts after six quarters.
  • The return rate is 0.7%.
  • Mobile is 76% of traffic.
  • DocMorris reports that newer e-Rx cohorts are 4.5x more loyal than paper-Rx customers, with order frequency of 4.1 times a year.
  • Six-month repeat cycles reduce friction.
  • Kantar: more than 30% of Germans have bought medicine online, and 44% of 30-39-year-olds do so regularly.

Fragile:

  • Active German Rx customers grew only from 1.67m to 1.73m in Q2 2026. Total active customers rose 0.5m in Q2 against 0.7m in Q1. Rx revenue growth is increasingly driven by revenue per customer rather than by customer count.
  • App downloads were down 18% year on year in September 2026, despite a 21% month-on-month rebound. Active app users fell 3% in August.
  • CardLink awareness is only 35% (Deutsche Bank survey). About 80% of Germans are unaware of digital redemption.
  • Patients aged 55+ resist ordering Rx online, and Germany's pharmacy density is high (17 km² per pharmacy against Sweden's 285 km²).
  • Acute demand is structurally lost to local pharmacies because of next-day delivery.

The judgement that matters for the model. German Rx revenue equals Rx customers times revenue per customer, and the second term carries 2026. Revenue per active German Rx customer rose by roughly 35-40% year on year, as Rx revenue grew about 58% while Rx customers grew about 16% (1.50m to 1.74m, the transcript figure). That comes from frequency, specialty mix and the six-month cycle. It cannot compound indefinitely.

From 2027, German Rx growth will track Rx customer growth (about 15-20% a year at the current acquisition pace), plus Fixum, plus modest frequency gains. Unless acquisition re-accelerates through PoPP, Health ID, the co-payment rise or new marketing, German Rx growth converges towards the low 20s by late 2027. That is still above the roughly 15-20% that consensus implies.

Monitorables: quarterly German Rx customer count; app monthly active users (a sequential fall of more than 10% a month is the Risks report's trigger); repeat rate below 90% (the early warning in Business Foundations).

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

A701st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Regulators are helping materially through fees, digital rails and legal certainty. Political actors are split: the minister is neutral, the pharmacists' lobby hostile, and the cost-containment commission a latent threat. The position is helpful but structurally fragile, because the profit is state-set.

Helping:

  • Fixum €9.00 (July 2026) and €9.50 (January 2027).
  • CardLink to March 2028, with the option to extend.
  • PoPP specification (September 2026), removing SMS and enabling Health ID.
  • Six-month repeat cycles.
  • The Health Ministry abandoned stricter temperature controls (June 2026).
  • BGH rulings (July and November 2025) protect bonuses and expose chambers to damages.
  • The Bundestag research service doubts a mail-order ban is compatible with EU law.
  • Health Minister Linnemann rejects an online/offline divide.
  • The EU's cross-border e-Rx agenda.

Hurting:

  • The statutory rebate rises to €2.07.
  • The signature requirement (about €0.19 per delivery).
  • The Karlsruhe ruling restricts vouchers linked to OTC.
  • Linnemann's criticism of bonuses used to offset co-payments; any legislative response "would take significant time" (Deutsche Bank).
  • The Apothekertag's ban motions.
  • A legal study (Kalscheuer) argues a ban could be justified on supply-security grounds.
  • The Health Finance Commission (€42.3bn of 2027 savings, dynamic rebates).
  • In Switzerland, Federal Office of Public Health price cuts.

Fragility:

  • Low over 18 months. The changes are enacted and the minister is neutral.
  • Medium over three years. As online Rx share moves towards 3-5%, the lobby's pressure and insurers' cost-containment interest grow together. The most plausible adverse instrument is not a ban but a cap on or ban of Rx bonuses for foreign mail-order pharmacies (as in 2021), or a higher rebate.
  • Indicator to monitor: any draft bill touching "Rx-Boni" or the Apothekenreform follow-ups after 2027.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

A821st of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: The realistic addressable market is several times Redcare's current revenue. It permits German Rx growth of 20-30% a year for several years and group growth in the low-to-mid teens. The binding constraint is the pace of patient conversion, not the size of the market.

Markets:

  • German statutory Rx: about €55bn, growing about 4.2% a year (Jefferies).
  • Western European pharmacy market: €260bn (Morgan Stanley).
  • Swiss Rx: served through MediService; liberalisation no earlier than 2029-30.
  • German online OTC: already about 25-26% penetrated, so it grows roughly with the market and share.

Penetration and what it means for Redcare.

  • Today about 1-2% of German Rx is online. Redcare's FY26 German Rx of about €745m is about 1.35% of the market.
  • Jefferies' base case is low-to-mid single digits by FY28; its upside case is low double digits.
  • Experts' ceilings: 10% in the medium term (a former Shop Apotheke chief executive) against 20-25% within five years (another expert). Morgan Stanley: about 10% "eventually". Sweden's online Rx share is 12%.
  • Redcare's own impairment testing assumes 15% annual sales growth over eight years and models about 10% online Rx penetration "within a few years".

Arithmetic (illustrative, not a forecast).

  • At 4% online penetration in 2028 on a market of about €60bn, with Redcare at 60-67% of online, German Rx would be about €1.45-1.6bn. That is roughly double FY26 and implies about 40% a year.
  • At 10% by about 2031 on about €65bn, with a 60% share, it would be about €3.9bn. That is about 40% of today's entire group.

Mid-term growth allowed (my judgement).

  • German Rx about 25-35% a year for 2027-28, then around 20%.
  • Swiss Rx about 10% a year.
  • Non-Rx about 7-10% a year.
  • International about 10-15% a year.
  • Group about 13-17% a year. Consensus has 12.7% and 10.8% for FY27 and FY28.

The real ceiling is conversion pace. Active German Rx customers are 1.73m, against tens of millions of chronic statutory patients. The size of the market is not in question.

Overall external picture

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

B+702nd of 11

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Group revenue growth bottomed at +17.8% in Q4 2025 and has re-accelerated (+18.4% Q1, +20.3% Q2, about +20% in Q3 2026 by implication). This happened because German Rx accelerated while OTC slowed. Guidance and consensus assume a sharp deceleration from Q4 2026 into 2027. On the evidence that looks too steep, so the cycle is turning less than the forecasts imply.

The quarterly path.

Period Group revenue growth
Q4 2024 to Q3 2025 25-28%
Q4 2025 (lapping, OTC weakness) 17.8%
Q1 2026 18.4%
Q2 2026 20.3%
Q3 2026 about 20% by implication

What the guidance implies for Q4 2026. FY26 guidance of 16-18% implies H2 growth of about 13% and Q4 growth of 8-15% (Deutsche Bank). For German Rx, guidance of €730-760m against about €551m in the first nine months implies Q4 German Rx of about €180-210m. That is roughly +17-37% against a Q4 2025 base of about €150m, which was itself already lifted by the Rx bonus launched in late 2025. September alone grew 51%.

Reading. Management deliberately guides to a lapping cliff. The Q4 2025 bonus launch does raise the base, but a fall from +51% in September to roughly +25% in Q4 would need a very abrupt break.

Consensus for 2027. Group +12.7%. On my rough segment build (non-Rx +8-10%, Swiss Rx +10-15%), consensus implies German Rx growth of only about 15-20% a year to FY28. That would take German Rx to about €1.0-1.05bn in FY28, about 1.8% of the German Rx market for Redcare. Brokers' own base case is low-to-mid single-digit online penetration by FY28 at a 67% Redcare share of online. That is internally inconsistent with consensus revenue: consensus revenue sits below the brokers' own penetration narrative.

Turning-point risks.

  • Active German Rx customers grew only about 60-70 thousand in Q2 2026 (1.67m to 1.73m). Growth is coming from frequency and basket, not new patients.
  • App downloads were down 18% year on year in September 2026.
  • German Rx growth will mechanically halve as the base doubles. Even so, a 2027 run-rate of 30% or more in H1, settling to the 20s in H2, fits continued conversion of chronic cohorts better than consensus does.

Implication for the model. Base German Rx growth for 2027 should sit above consensus. Group growth for 2027 should be in the mid-teens rather than 12.7%. OTC is the swing factor.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

A741st of 11

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Tailwinds are strong, structural and mostly regulation-dated over 2026-28. Headwinds are moderate and concentrated in OTC consumer demand, the cost of competition for patients, and the market's discount rate. The external picture is the most favourable in the company's history for its core German Rx engine. The main caveat is that the tailwinds are state-granted and the market regime is hostile to the stock's profile.

Driver by driver (strength; direction):

  1. German e-Rx channel shift: very strong; tailwind; accelerating; 2+ years of visibility.
  2. Pharmacy remuneration (Fixum, rebate): strong; net tailwind of €9.5-13.3m EBITDA in 2027 rising to €12.3-17.2m in 2028 (Deutsche Bank); enacted.
  3. Regulatory access (CardLink to March 2028, PoPP, six-month cycles, BGH bonus ruling): strong; tailwind; transition risk concentrated in Q4 2027 to Q1 2028.
  4. Co-payment rise 2027: a two-sided driver. It raises demand for online bonuses, and so raises the cost of acquiring and retaining patients. Net mildly positive if the duopoly stays rational.
  5. Ageing and chronic therapy mix: moderate; tailwind; slow.
  6. OTC and beauty consumer: moderate headwind. The market is saturated online (about 25%) and price-transparent, with drugstore entrants.
  7. Competition: neutral, tilting to a mild headwind as DocMorris recovers and Rossmann enters.
  8. Labour and logistics costs: a mild headwind, neutralised from 2027 by automation.
  9. Political lobby: a latent headwind. It is low in probability but high in magnitude, and it grows as online share grows.
  10. Market regime (rates, risk-off, momentum and fragility tilts): a headwind to the multiple, not to earnings.

By level.

  • Value chain: the strongest tailwind. Value migrates from local pharmacies and wholesalers to scaled online dispensers.
  • Industry (online pharmacy): strong tailwind. Consolidation is under way and sub-scale players are exiting.
  • Sector (B.8 Healthcare distribution and retail): neutral. The sector digest names "German prescriptions moving online" as the sector's strongest story, rated "big".

Overall. For the 18-month model, external forces add to both the volume and the per-unit economics of the profit driver (German Rx). That is rare. The Consumer should lean revenue and gross profit assumptions above consensus for German Rx and at or slightly below guidance for OTC, and should carry an explicit regulatory downside scenario.

Internal change forces/risks

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Redcare is in the middle of a deliberate, owner-led transition from a pharmacy land-grab to a profitability and cash compounder. The rebasing was done in March 2026 under a new finance director. Two upgrades followed. Marketing intensity was cut by 210bp while Rx growth accelerated. The automation that underpins the 2027 margin step is funded and on schedule.

The positive changes are strong and well-resourced. The negative is concentrated in people: a three-week-old chief executive with no pharmacy background, a vacant commercial post and high warehouse turnover, entering a 2027 that combines co-payment competition, Sevenum commissioning and preparation for PoPP.

For the model, the internal forces support:

  • operating leverage of about 8-10% on incremental revenue;
  • marketing at about 5% of revenue;
  • capex below 2% of revenue from FY27;
  • no M&A and no shareholder returns before FY28.

The CMD on 12 November is the single event that could re-weight the margin path.

Internal risks

Group judgement

Risks

D388th of 11

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is bimodal:

  • Fundamental risks are concentrated, low-probability and high-impact, and are mostly in the state's hands: bonus restrictions, rebates, the PoPP transition and, in the extreme, a ban.
  • Company-controlled risks cluster in the next six months: Sevenum commissioning in Q1 2027, the new chief executive's CMD and co-payment competition.
  • Perception risk is high: a 62.6% six-month rally into a risk-off, anti-momentum, anti-fragility regime, with 17% short interest, thin liquidity and monthly trackers that turn noise into narrative.

The core growth engine has not decelerated. Only OTC has, and that is the honest answer to C-80.

The fair trough is about 30-40% below the price, and the stock has visited the severe case within the past year. For the Consumer: build an explicit downside scenario combining a bonus restriction or slower Rx acquisition (German Rx growth near 20% in 2027, falling to the mid-teens) with marketing back at 6%. Weight it lightly over 18 months, more over three years.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

B652nd of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Standards are clearly improving in cost discipline, disclosure and governance, and execution on the big projects is on time. Labour retention and systems scalability have improved less.

Improving:

  • Cost and marketing. Marketing -210bp to 5.1%; adjusted selling and distribution expenses 16.4% of revenue in H1 2026 against 18.9% in H1 2025; both segments cut marketing by about 200bp.
  • Projects. Sevenum on schedule for Q1 2027 commissioning. Pilsen went live in October/November 2025; its early Austrian supply disruption was fixed by early 2026.
  • International. From -6.2% margin in FY23 to break-even in H1 2026.
  • Disclosure. First marketing ratio, cohort economics, NPS (74 overall, e-Rx 77 in Q2 and 80 in August 2026) and repeat rate (90%).
  • Governance.
  • A dedicated internal audit function was set up in 2025, curing a Dutch code non-compliance.
  • A Speak Up line was set up in October 2024.
  • The supervisory board is 80% independent.
  • Remuneration is long-term only, with no short-term cash bonus. It carries 20% ESG weighting and Dutch-law clawback.
  • Structure. The Europa Apotheek entities were merged into Shop-Apotheke on 1 January 2026, and nu3 was centralised at a cost of €1.9m.
  • Pricing discipline. AI pricing rolled out after the Austrian test.
  • Working capital. Inventory forecasting is a stated focus.

Not improving or weak:

  • Warehouse turnover rose to 39.1%, against a 25-30% sector base.
  • Retail media systems could not keep up with demand.
  • Leadership churn.
  • Adjusted EBITDA excludes ESOP cost (€1.4m in H1).
  • The end of preliminary quarterly sales releases reduces visibility, even if it reduces noise.

Peer reference. DocMorris is improving faster from a lower base: an "AI-First" programme with CHF 15m of savings, closing the Ludwigshafen warehouse, and break-even in Q3 2026. Redcare's standards are better in absolute terms, but its improvement gap is narrowing.

C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

C+403rd of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Six negative developments are present or plausible. The two that matter most for the 18-month model are a re-weighting of the plan at the CMD by the new chief executive, and the cost of defending Rx share through the 2027 co-payment rise.

  1. Leadership discontinuity and a pharma-expertise gap. Chief executive, finance director and commercial director all changed within 12 months. Neither the chief executive nor the finance director has pharmacy experience; the commercial director's post is vacant. The risk is missteps in regulatory negotiation (PoPP, rebate politics, bonus advertising law) and in commercial execution.
  2. CMD re-weighting. Plausible: higher reinvestment in marketplace, AI, telemedicine or marketing; a slower path to 5%; or new targets on a different basis (EPS). Given March's rebase, a re-weighting is more likely than a cut.
  3. Bonus and co-payment escalation. If DocMorris or Rossmann push co-payment cover in 2027, Redcare's undisclosed bonus cost could absorb a meaningful part of the €9.5-13.3m Fixum gain.
  4. Working capital creep. FactSet NWC rose from €35m (Q2 2025) to €114m (Q2 2026), with inventory up 32% to €213m. Rx receivables from insurers take over a month. Supplier terms are already stretched to 33 days, and wholesale purchase conditions are under discussion.
  5. Thinner disclosure and adjusted metrics. Preliminary quarterly sales releases have ended. ESOP cost is excluded from adjusted EBITDA. Cohort churn, co-payment spend and retail media profit remain undisclosed. All of this makes the "false friend" pattern (growth bought with promotions) harder to detect; this was the Risks report's winning pre-mortem hypothesis.
  6. Labour and operational transition risk. Sevenum commissioning in Q1 2027, the peak flu season, collides with 39% warehouse turnover. The 2021 Sevenum migration caused a sales-outlook cut and a Q3 2021 sales miss of about 9% against consensus.

Skew: none of these is the base case, but item 3 and item 6 are the most likely to cost a quarter.

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

C585th of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: Moderately congruent. Goals, incentives, strategy and capital allocation now line up around "profitable Rx scale". The operating culture shows strain at the warehouse floor and in leadership churn, and the system is not yet a Lifco- or Ryanair-style integrated machine.

Congruent elements:

  • Incentives. Long-term only, with no short-term cash bonus. The 2023 plan's stock options vest after three years on revenue growth plus adjusted EBITDA margin, with 20% on ESG (emissions per order) and Dutch-law clawback. That aligns with "growth with margin". Caveat: adjusted EBITDA excludes ESOP cost, and EPS, now the guidance focus, is not yet a vesting metric (not stated).
  • Strategy and capital both point to Rx scale, automation and monetisation.
  • Culture statements: "Because We Care"; a flat organisation empowering the expert level; the RISE career programme; Udemy access.
  • Customer outcomes: NPS 74-80, repeat 90%, return rate 0.7%. Service culture shows in the numbers.
  • Governance: 80% independent supervisory board, single share class.

Incongruent elements:

  • Warehouse floor. Turnover rose to 39.1% (bottom quartile), while group turnover was 26.9%.
  • Pay versus outcomes. CEO pay rose 21% (to €1.13m) in a year when market capitalisation fell 52%; the CEO had not met the shareholding requirement by late 2025.
  • Leadership churn.
  • The diversity target was missed: 20% women on the supervisory board.

The congruence test that matters. Do the stated "customer trust first" (no OTC price rises) and "EPS and cash focus" conflict in 2027, when co-payment cover becomes expensive? How management resolves that will reveal the true culture.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

D427th of 11

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: Seven such risks are worth carrying. Probabilities are my judgement over the next three years; magnitudes are relative to the FY27-28 EBITDA path.

  1. Legislative restriction of Rx bonuses or co-payment cover for foreign mail-order pharmacies (a repeat of the 2021 bonus ban). - Plausibility: low-medium (about 10%). The BGH and EU law protect bonuses, but the minister criticised them and the lobby is active. - Magnitude: large. It removes the main acquisition tool against local pharmacies, slows German Rx conversion and forces marketing spend back up. German Rx growth could halve. The 2021 precedent coincided with growth falling to 7.3%.
  2. A mail-order Rx ban. - Plausibility: very low (under 3%). Significant doubts under EU law (Bundestag research service); "non-binding motions". - Magnitude: existential for German Rx (about 22-28% of revenue and most of the equity value of growth).
  3. A failure in the PoPP or Health ID transition at the CardLink sunset (March 2028), with no further extension. - Plausibility: low (about 5-10%). Gematik retains the option to extend, and Phase 1 went live on an accelerated schedule. - Magnitude: a sharp drop in redemptions for one to two quarters, and slower acquisition of older patients.
  4. A Sevenum commissioning failure in Q1 2027, the peak flu season. - Plausibility: low-medium (about 10-15%). The 2021 migration precedent; 39% warehouse turnover; software-integration risk. - Magnitude: one or two quarters of lost sales and extra cost. In 2021 Q3 sales missed by about 9% against consensus and the outlook was cut.
  5. Cyber attack, data breach or a power or IT outage at the single Rx hub. - Plausibility: low (about 5%). - Magnitude: weeks of lost Rx fulfilment, regulatory fines and reputational damage. CVS lost £7m of revenue to its 2024 cyber incident.
  6. A cost-containment shock: a larger statutory rebate increase or a Fixum clawback under the Health Finance Commission's €42bn savings agenda. - Plausibility: medium-low (about 15%). - Magnitude: each €0.10 per pack is about €1.3-1.8m a year; €0.50 would erase most of the 2027 Fixum gain.
  7. An adverse reset of wholesaler purchase terms or supplier credit (currently under discussion). - Plausibility: medium-low. - Magnitude: a few million euros of EBITDA, plus a working capital outflow if payment terms shorten. Payables are €252m: one week of shortening is about €50m of cash.

Correlated cluster (the Risks report's meta-analysis). Margin compression from Rx mix plus an OTC price war plus a competitive entrant can coincide. The Risks report's FALSE FRIEND pre-mortem wins on fewest inconsistencies. I note the Wisdom Library warning that "the vaguest explanation wins a fewest-contradictions contest". The Q2 and Q3 margin rise at 3.5% is the hard evidence against it.

Net: the risk set is dominated by the state (items 1, 2, 3 and 6). The company controls items 4 and 5.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

–45

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The drivers are diffuse for revenue and concentrated for profit. The whole difference between the forecast years and the history, in EPS and in free cash flow, comes from one variable, the EBITDA margin, crossing a fixed layer of costs; and the margin itself turns on three nodes, each of which caused a miss in the past. That concentration lowers predictability. Its compensation is that every node is dated and observable.

The differences to be explained. Revenue growth halves (about 25% a year in FY20-FY25 to about 12.5% in FY26-FY30). The margin moves from an average of about 1.5%, swinging by up to three points a year, to a steady rise from 2.9% to 4.85%. EPS and free cash flow turn from persistently negative to positive. The share count stops rising.

Revenue: diffuse. Of the increase from FY26 to FY27 (about €465m), German Rx provides about 45-48%, DACH non-Rx about 24%, International about 16% and Swiss Rx about 13%. No single segment decides the outcome, and German Rx and non-Rx respond to unrelated forces.

Gross profit: two legs, not one. The narrative calls German Rx the engine, but because its gross margin is about 14%, its extra revenue in FY27 brings about €40m of gross profit including the fee, against about €55m from non-Rx. At the level of contribution after fulfilment and marketing the two are about equal. The forecast therefore depends on the non-Rx half holding its margin as much as on Rx volume, which the narrative understates.

EBITDA to EPS: one variable. In FY27 the charges below EBITDA are close to fixed: about €66m of depreciation and €17m of net financial items on the model basis, and €99m including the amortisation of acquired intangibles and stock options on the reported-like basis. EPS is therefore a claim on the margin above a hurdle.

  • On the model basis the hurdle is a margin of about 2.1%; the forecast of 3.55% leaves 145bp.
  • On the reported-like basis the hurdle is about 2.5%; the forecast leaves 105bp.
  • Each 10bp of margin is about €3.9m of EBITDA and about €0.16 of EPS, which is 7% of model-basis EPS and 10% of reported-like EPS. A margin of 3.2% instead of 3.55% cuts the first by about a quarter and the second by about a third.

The margin: three nodes and one mechanical stream.

  1. The commercial give-back: marketing, Rx bonuses and co-payment cover, and non-Rx price. This is one decision cluster, taken by management quarter by quarter in response to DocMorris and to non-Rx demand. It is the most concentrated driver. Each 25bp of revenue is about €10m, or 7% of FY27 EBITDA, and its components have swung by 65bp to 210bp of revenue within single quarters in 2026.
  2. Sevenum. One project: about €8m in FY27 in the base case, about €19m if management's 50bp is taken literally, and a cost of €3-8m if a quarter is lost.
  3. The state. The fee is enacted and worth about €7m in FY27. The legality of the co-payment waiver is the binary element.
  4. Volume through the fixed-cost network and the layering of repeat customers. This is the diffuse, mechanical stream, worth about 40-45bp a year at 13% growth.

The link to history. Each past miss came from one of the three nodes: the give-back in FY24 and Q4 2025, logistics in 2021, the state in 2021 (the bonus ban). The forecast differs from the history not because new drivers appear but because the same three nodes are assumed to behave. That is the most compact description of the risk in the forecasts.

The base rate. Forecasts that rest on one or two discrete drivers are usually met late, not on time: Apotea's Varberg hub, Meds' warehouse move (profit warning, 22 October 2026), and Medios' warning after a German reform (July 2026). Redcare's base case leans on two discrete drivers for a little under half of the gross margin gain in FY27: the fee (about 17bp), which is certain, and Sevenum (about 20bp), which is not.

A detail on the fee that changes the quarterly shape. Net of the higher rebate (€0.30 a pack) and the signature rule (€0.19 a delivery, about €0.10 a pack), the January 2027 step is worth only about €0.10 a pack against H2 2026. Nearly all of the FY27 year-on-year gain, about €6m of €7m, is the July 2026 step running through the first half. From Q3 2027 the fee gives almost no year-on-year help, so the margin gains forecast for H2 2027 (about 50bp) must come entirely from Sevenum and the mechanical stream. H2 2027 is thus the first clean test of the margin thesis.

2b

Internal corporate change forces

13 answers
C-1160C-1268C-1364C-1465C-1550C-1660C-1762C-1850C-1942C-2070C-8762C-2168C-2276
C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

C604th of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Change is near-total and fast. Within twelve months the chief executive, the finance director, the commercial director, the supervisory board chair and three supervisory directors have all changed.

What changed:

  • Chief executive. Olaf Heinrich, about three years in post and with twelve years before that at DocMorris, stepped down on 1 October 2026; the change was announced on 28 August 2026 and surprised the market. He was succeeded by Peter Schmid von Linstow, who has twenty years scaling marketplaces at AutoScout24, eBay Germany and Visable (where he sold majority control to Alibaba in 2023). He had sat on the supervisory board, as vice-chair, since April 2026, and was formally appointed on 14 October 2026.
  • Finance director. Hendrik Krampe, with eight years as an Amazon European marketplace finance director, joined in December 2025 and was formally appointed in April 2026, succeeding Jasper Eenhorst. He worked with the new chief executive at eBay Germany (2006-09).
  • Commercial director. Dirk Brüse left in April 2026. The post is vacant, and Heinrich covered it until he left.
  • Supervisory board. At the April 2026 AGM the chair passed from Björn Söder to co-founder Michael Köhler, who controls about 8.3%. Jaska de Bakker and Jérôme Cochet left; Anja Hendel, Max Müller and Schmid von Linstow joined.
  • Continuity. Theresa Holler, the chief operating officer and chief pharmacist with more than ten years on the board, stays. Heinrich advises until April 2027.
  • Shareholders. Galenica holds 10.4% and MK Beleggingsmaatschappij 8.3%. There is no activist.

What it signals, and this is the judgement that matters. The founder-chaired board has deliberately replaced a pharmacy and regulatory operator with a two-person marketplace-monetisation team. That implies the board believes the regulatory and land-grab phase is substantially won: the BGH bonus ruling, CardLink to 2028, the enacted Fixum increases and 67% online Rx share. The next value source is, in its view, platform economics: retail media, marketplace commission, own brands, AI pricing, operating leverage and EPS.

This matches the "CFO refresh as leading indicator of a capital-discipline reset" pattern in the Wisdom Library. Krampe's arrival preceded the March 2026 rebasing, the switch from EBITDA to EPS guidance, the first disclosure of marketing ratios and the end of preliminary quarterly sales releases.

The two risks the signal carries.

  1. Loss of pharmaceutical and regulatory depth as the 2027-28 PoPP transition and co-payment politics peak. The shares fell more than 15% on the announcement for this reason, then recovered more than 40% in September.
  2. New-CEO behaviour at the 12 November CMD. New chief executives often reset targets or raise investment. Here a reset is less likely, because guidance was already rebased in March 2026 under the same finance director. A re-weighting towards investment in marketplace and AI, at the expense of the near-term margin path, is plausible.

Peer contrast. DocMorris' leadership faced activist attack (CEPD and Pelion). CVS shows the opposite case: a retiring finance-trained chief executive, a thin bench, and a market waiting for clarity.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

B683rd of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The change is material and largely resourced. Redcare has pivoted from buying growth to harvesting operating leverage, while keeping Rx share leadership as the engine. It is well articulated in numbers; it is not yet articulated by the new chief executive.

The pivot in practice:

  • Marketing moved from TV to targeted online acquisition and direct Rx cash bonuses: 5.1% of revenue in Q2 2026 (-210bp), with management saying "peak marketing is behind us" and intensity will settle at about 5%.
  • Cost leadership through Sevenum automation (€100m, Q1 2027, 70% lower labour per order) and Pilsen wage arbitrage. Capex falls below 2% of revenue from FY27, and no further major capacity investment is needed for five years.
  • AI pricing to balance OTC volume and margin.
  • Higher-margin revenue lines: marketplace commission (under 5% of sales), retail media (more than 150 pharmaceutical suppliers), own brands.
  • An app-first funnel. CardLink is in-app only, and app users are more loyal.
  • A shift in investor framing from EBITDA to EPS and free cash flow: "scale must translate into cash generation"; a "predictable cash-flow compounder".
  • International moved to break-even: 0.9% margin and €1.4m in Q2 2026, with three of four markets profitable and France the exception.

How well it is resourced. Funded: Sevenum capex is largely spent (€17m in H1 2026, €13m in H2 2026), the €300m convertible extends debt to 2032, cash is €119m, and free cash flow break-even is expected in FY27. Two gaps remain:

  • Commercial leadership. The commercial director's post is vacant.
  • Systems. Retail media systems "failed to scale fast enough" to meet supplier demand (Q1 2026 call).

How well it is articulated. The Q2 2026 disclosures were the best in the company's history: cohort economics, the marketing ratio, segment margins and explicit H2 moderation. What is missing is the new chief executive's own strategy, due at the 12 November CMD.

Wisdom Library check. "Growth funded by incentives cannot fund margin" is the test. The pivot claims that Rx bonus spend is an investment with 2x gross profit cohorts, not a subsidy. The evidence so far (Q2 margin up while Rx grew 58%) supports that claim. The 2027 co-payment rise will test it.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

B642nd of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Near-term guidance has been raised twice in 2026, but only on revenue. The mid-term margin target was cut in March 2026 and the long-term target maintained. Net: upward momentum from a deliberately lowered base. The CMD on 12 November is the next decision point.

The sequence:

  1. 4 March 2026, FY25 results. A "painful expectations reset". FY26 targets were "well below expectations" (Jefferies). The mid-term adjusted EBITDA target moved from 8% to 5%; Deutsche Bank and Krampe frame 5% as "within three years", that is by FY28. The long-term ambition above 8% stayed. EBITDA guidance beyond the next year was discontinued in favour of EPS. Deutsche Bank cut its adjusted EBITDA forecasts by 23%.
  2. 15 June 2026, ad hoc. Sales growth raised to 15-17% from 13-15%. German Rx raised from more than €670m to €680-720m.
  3. 28 September 2026, ad hoc. Sales growth raised to 16-18%. German Rx raised to €730-760m (+45-51%). Non-Rx growth held at 10-12%. The adjusted EBITDA margin held at 2.5-3.0% both times.

The pattern: revenue raised, margin never raised. H1 margin was 2.6% and Q3 about 3.5%. Hitting the top of the range (3.0% on €3.47bn = about €104m) needs a Q4 margin of only about 3%. Consensus (€99m, 2.9%) sits at the top of guidance.

The mid-term gap. Consensus FY28 EBITDA margin is 4.2%, below the company's 5% by 2028. The sell-side does not yet believe the mid-term target. That is the gap the CMD can close, or confirm.

Skew for the CMD. Positive on the margin path, because Fixum, Sevenum and marketing at 5% mechanically add about 80-100bp by 2027 to a Q2/Q3 run-rate of 3.5%. The risk is that a new marketplace chief executive frames a higher-investment, slower-margin plan.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

D506th of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Little perimeter reduction, and little is needed. Simplification is legal and administrative, not portfolio.

The record:

  • Entity mergers on 1 January 2026, with no change to the segments.
  • nu3 own-brand activities centralised.
  • The GoPuls quick-commerce venture written off; €13m of contingent consideration released.
  • No unit divested.

The one candidate for exit or rethink is France. It is the only International market "too small to scale", but it is also the market most likely to benefit if French e-Rx rules liberalise. With International now at break-even, management has little reason to exit, and the new chief executive could decide either way.

Investment meaning. The perimeter is already narrow: one dominant market (Germany), one joint venture (Switzerland) and four small markets. Perimeter change is not a lever for the model. Its absence is mildly positive for predictability and neutral for value.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

D606th of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Modest, capital-light and sensible. The growth is product depth within existing markets, not new geography.

Geography:

  • Austria is now served from Pilsen.
  • A Czech online pharmacy launch was delayed by manufacturers refusing direct supply deals.
  • Switzerland's expansion runs through the redcare.ch assortment and readiness for a 2029-30 liberalisation, inside the Galenica joint venture.
  • EU-wide cross-border e-Rx (European Health Data Space, 2029) is advocated by the chief operating officer. It is an option, not a plan.

Product and business model:

  • A third-party marketplace with commission revenue.
  • Retail media for more than 150 suppliers, with daily campaign analytics that experts rate above DocMorris'.
  • Own brands, growing faster than the group.
  • "NOW!" same-day delivery through partner local pharmacies, which is the answer to acute demand.
  • B2B wholesale (AustPharma, bought for €0.3m in 2024 for an Austrian wholesale licence).
  • Digital health tools (smartpatient, MedApp, bought in 2021).

Gap: no owned telemedicine funnel, against DocMorris' TeleClinic. That is the most conspicuous product hole, and an acquisition candidate for a marketplace chief executive.

Sensibility. High. Each adjacency monetises existing traffic and supplier relationships at high incremental margin and needs little capital. The failed GoPuls venture shows the company can mis-step in convenience formats.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

A621st of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Real price increases exist, but they are state-granted on Rx, not company-driven. On OTC the company is deliberately a price-giver.

German Rx.

  • The Fixum rises 13.8% in total, in two steps: +7.8% in July 2026 and +5.6% in January 2027.
  • Net of the higher rebate, the fixed element per pack rises about 13% from H1 2026 to 2027.
  • That is well above wage inflation and is real.
  • It is latent for 2027 (the full-year effect) and was partly realised in H2 2026, worth about €3.1-4.4m in H2 2026 per Deutsche Bank.
  • Management increasingly highlights it as a structural earnings driver.

OTC.

  • Management says it "will not increase OTC prices if it compromises customer trust".
  • It prices up to 10% below rivals on key items.
  • AI pricing tools are used to optimise mix and margin. They added about 2 percentage points to German non-Rx growth, and the German non-Rx gross margin rose more than 2 points quarter on quarter in Q2 2026. That is margin management through price-mix optimisation, not list-price increases.

Swiss Rx. The Federal Office of Public Health cuts prices; the price trend there is negative.

Hidden negative price. Rx bonuses and co-payment cover are negative prices paid to patients. Their scale is undisclosed, and the 2027 co-payment rise may raise them.

Net realised price/mix: positive in 2027, driven by Fixum.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

D507th of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: It is not stepping up. M&A is dormant, and nothing suggests a change before the CMD.

The record is mixed.

  • MediService (51%, May 2023). A clear success. It added about €307m of Rx sales at consolidation and €566m in FY25. Galenica called the partnership an "absolute full success". It diluted the group gross margin (28.2% to 25.2%), but earns profitable, cash-generative Swiss Rx volume.
  • GoPuls. A failure, written down.
  • smartpatient and MedApp (2021). Small digital tools; impact unclear.
  • AustPharma (2024, €0.3m). A tactical licence purchase.
  • Farmaline / Europa Apotheek (historic). Integrated; the brands have been consolidated.

What could change. A marketplace-oriented chief executive could buy a telemedicine or digital-health asset to close the gap with TeleClinic. Distressed small German mail-order players (Disapo, Sanicare) offer customer lists cheaply, but Redcare wins those customers organically anyway.

The balance sheet allows bolt-ons, not transformational deals. Cash is €119m and free cash flow is negative in FY26.

Model implication: assume no M&A spend.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

F429th of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: No. Buybacks are explicitly ruled out "until the business reaches consistent structural net profitability". No dividend has ever been paid.

Recent balance-sheet use was defensive refinancing, not returns:

  • the €300m 2032 convertible (1.75% coupon, 110% redemption) was issued in April 2025;
  • €157.9m of the 2028 convertibles were repurchased;
  • the final €64.5m was redeemed on 21 January 2026.

This raised finance expense in 2026, and the effective tax rate in H1 2026 reached 41.2% on deferred tax effects.

Leverage. Comfortable on the company's definition (1.1x) and long-dated. The only capital commitment ahead is ordinary capex below 2% of revenue from FY27.

Plausible timing for returns. Not before FY28. That needs consistent net profit (consensus €22m in FY27 and €56m in FY28) and positive free cash flow (consensus €28m in FY27, €64m in FY28). A former Amazon finance director could make a buyback the 2028 narrative. Market capitalisation is small relative to future free cash flow if margins reach 5%.

The convertible is the open capital-structure question. Its conversion price is not in the sources. At a higher share price it becomes equity dilution, roughly 10-15% of the share count if converted, depending on the conversion price. The Consumer should obtain the conversion terms; this is requested in T-P4.

Model implication: no returns before FY28.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–70

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Five further positive forces are present or plausible. Together they raise the ceiling on the mid-term margin.

  1. Monetisation of traffic under marketplace-native management. Retail media and marketplace commission carry high incremental margins, though the scale is unsegmented. At DocMorris, retail media earns "mid-double-digit EBITDA margins". Even 1% of revenue at that margin would add about 50bp to group EBITDA. This is the most plausible upside the new team brings.
  2. Cohort compounding. 2025 German Rx cohorts produced about 4x the revenue and 2x the accumulated gross profit of non-Rx cohorts after six quarters. The mixed-order rate (an Rx order carrying at least one non-Rx item) was 40.8% in late 2025. Each year's cohort adds recurring gross profit that needs no new marketing.
  3. International moving from break-even towards DACH-like margins. The DACH segment earned about €28m EBITDA in Q2 2026 on €696m of revenue (about 4%). International at 0.9% has 2-3 points of potential on €0.6bn of revenue: €12-18m of EBITDA over time.
  4. Free cash flow inflection in FY27. Capex roughly halves as a share of revenue, from about 4% in FY25 to below 2%. This changes how the stock is valued (the multiple, not just the earnings) and opens returns or bolt-ons.
  5. Index and sentiment repair. Possible STOXX 600 re-inclusion, short covering on 17% short interest, and the end of convertible arbitrage pressure.

There is also a technical tailwind for EPS: tax losses carried forward probably shelter early profits. This is not disclosed and is inferred from consensus tax charges.

C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

A621st of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The controlling actors have already acted, not merely indicated. The founder-chair has engineered the leadership change. The strategic shareholder supports the status quo, and no activist is present.

  • Michael Köhler (co-founder; 8.3% via a private holding company; supervisory board chair since April 2026; chairs the nomination committee and sits on audit) led the board refresh and chose a marketplace chief executive from his own board. That is the clearest indicator of intended change: monetisation and platform discipline over pharmacy operations. His June 2026 off-market transfer of €772,800 of shares was a family reorganisation, not a sale.
  • Galenica (10.4%; partner in the 51/49 MediService joint venture; receives MediService dividends of €5.1m in 2025) praises the partnership, sees Swiss liberalisation as an opportunity "due to its joint venture with Redcare", and gives no sign of seeking change. A longer-dated optionality question is whether Galenica would ever buy out or sell its 49%, or its 10.4%.
  • Smallcap World Fund holds about 5%. The free float is about 79%.
  • No activist (contrast DocMorris: CEPD and Pelion, with a 15% stake).
  • Short sellers hold about 17% of shares. Part is convertible hedging. This is a sentiment actor, not a controlling one.

Implication: governance stability is high, and strategic intent is clear and owner-driven.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

B+681st of 11

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Strong, broad and largely funded, with one execution question: a brand-new leadership team. The changes span:

  • cost: marketing at about 5%, automation, Pilsen and entity mergers;
  • revenue quality: Rx cohorts, AI pricing, retail media, marketplace and own brands;
  • segments: International at break-even;
  • capital: capex peak passed, refinancing done, free cash flow break-even in FY27;
  • communication: EPS framing, cohort disclosure and conservative guidance.

Each carries a number and a date. The resourcing is complete for the physical plan and incomplete for people: the commercial director's post is vacant, warehouse turnover is high and retail media systems are lagging.

Compared with the last 12-18 months. That period was a promotional land-grab that compressed margin (1.6% in Q4 2025) and ended in a credibility reset. The last six months show the change working: a 10-year high margin of 3.5% in Q2 and Q3 2026, alongside +56-58% German Rx growth. Growth and margin rose together, which is the signature of genuine operating leverage rather than a cut in investment.

Wisdom Library fit. "Sector tailwind plus a self-improving company" is the configuration. The external Rx shift is the tailwind; the cost and monetisation programme is the self-improvement. The open question is whether the new chief executive is a multiplier of this programme or a re-planner of it.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

A761st of 11

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: The staircase is unusually well-populated and well-dated for the next 18 months. Most steps are mechanical or regulatory and so well-founded. The novel items are the new chief executive's platform agenda and the first positive earnings and free cash flow in the company's 25-year history. The 18-36-month steps are weaker and carry the regulatory transition.

Next 6 months (to April 2027):
