Q3 2026 results 29 October 2026 Low (preliminary figures known) High. The margin is expected around 3.5% (flat on Q2 per the 28 September update), alongside German Rx +56%; a near-certain confirmation of guidance's top end
Capital Markets Day 12 November 2026 High. The new chief executive's first strategy statement; possibly mid-term targets reframed on EPS and free cash flow; possibly new KPI and segment disclosure (marketplace, retail media, German Rx customers) Medium. Two-sided: a re-weighting towards investment is the risk
Monthly gematik and app-tracker data through the period Low Positive skew, given seasonally strong Q4 and Q1
Fixum to €9.50 and co-payment rise 1 January 2027 Medium High (enacted). Expect brokers to roll the full-year Fixum benefit into FY27

Sevenum commissioning Q1 2027 Medium Medium. On schedule so far; peak-season execution risk
Rossmann launch late 2026 A negative step to absorb, not a positive

Months 6 to 18 (April 2027 to April 2028):

  1. The first full year of positive EPS (FY27 consensus €1.32) and positive free cash flow (consensus €28m). High novelty for the stock; well-founded on mechanics.
  2. Sevenum labour savings visible in H2 2027 margins (about +50bp). Medium novelty; well-founded.
  3. Margin approaching 4% in run-rate terms; consensus FY27 is 3.5%. Medium novelty.
  4. Appointment of a commercial director and possibly a platform or retail media leader. Low novelty.
  5. Possible STOXX 600 re-inclusion as market capitalisation recovers. Medium novelty, uncertain.
  6. PoPP Phase 2 rollout start in Q4 2027 with Health ID: a step change in onboarding friction. High novelty; moderately founded; carries transition risk.
  7. German online Rx penetration crossing roughly 2.5-3% on gematik and company data. Medium novelty.

Months 18 to 36 (2028 to 2029):

  1. The 5% EBITDA margin target year (FY28); consensus 4.2%. Medium novelty.
  2. The CardLink sunset (31 March 2028) and full PoPP migration. A risk event more than a positive.
  3. Capital returns become possible once there is "consistent structural net profitability" (FY28 consensus net income €56m). Buybacks or a dividend would be high novelty for this stock; foundation is medium.
  4. EU-wide cross-border e-prescriptions (EHDS, 2029). High novelty; low foundation for value.
  5. Swiss mail-order liberalisation (no earlier than 2029-30) through the Galenica joint venture. High novelty; low-medium foundation.
  6. The convertible becoming in the money, or being managed actively: a neutral-to-negative step.

Overall. Steps are frequent (at least quarterly), dated and mostly mechanical over 18 months. That is the profile the market rewards with a re-rating from EV/sales framing to P/E and free cash flow framing. Brokers note the stock sits at the 13th percentile of its own P/E history and at a discount to DocMorris on EV/sales.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

A721st of 11

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

A721st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure is close to ideal for a scaled online leader:

  • fixed regulated Rx prices with a rising per-pack fee;
  • a fragmented, chain-barred incumbent base that cannot pay bonuses or automate;
  • an online duopoly holding about 80%, with the leader at about 67%;
  • rising minimum efficient scale that is driving out the tail;
  • inelastic, recurring chronic demand.

Value is migrating along the chain towards Redcare. The Swedish precedent (27% online, consolidated around the most automated player) shows the destination.

The support has three fragilities, in order:

  1. Political. Rebates and bonus rules: low probability, high magnitude, rising with online share.
  2. Duopoly behaviour. A recapitalised DocMorris and the 2027 co-payment rise: medium probability, moderate magnitude.
  3. Acquisition. The slowing growth in new Rx patients, which makes 2027 German Rx growth depend on conversion catalysts (PoPP, co-payments) rather than on momentum alone.

For the model: hold Redcare's share of online Rx roughly stable to slightly lower, grow the online channel strongly, and carry the cost of competition in marketing and bonus spend rather than in price.

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?A721st of 11full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

B+641st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: On balance the intermediaries help. Doctors issue e-Rx, insurers pay the fixed fee, gematik provides the rails and carriers deliver. The fragilities are concentrated in gematik's technical transition and in insurer-side policy.

Intermediaries and their effect:

  • Prescribers. E-Rx issuance is near-universal (89-97% of statutory scripts). Some practices remain sceptical, but issuance is no longer the constraint. A prescribing-data lag of six months limits targeting.
  • Gematik and CardLink/PoPP (the decisive intermediary). CardLink is licensed to 31 March 2028. Redcare was first to adopt the new telematics-infrastructure access technology, which pulls prescriptions faster (April 2026). PoPP Phase 2 starts in Q4 2027, with Health ID biometrics and the EU Digital Identity Wallet. The CardLink technology is shared ("exact same basic technology", co-developed with DocMorris), so the rails are not a moat. They are a level playing field that favours the leader's scale.
  • Statutory insurers. They pay within about a month; receivables were €152.8m at H1 2026. They set the rebate (€2.07 from 2027). They are a potential threat if they partner with retail apps or if digital-referral rules change.
  • App platforms (Apple, Google). CardLink is in-app only, so traffic depends on app distribution. Web traffic is less relevant (Jefferies).
  • Carriers (DHL et al.). About €3 per package, plus the signature requirement, plus pick-up points and lockers.
  • Price-comparison sites (Medizinfuchs). These help acquisition and cap OTC pricing.
  • Telemedicine. DocMorris owns TeleClinic. Redcare depends on third-party teleprescribers (Zava), and that relationship is in litigation brought by local pharmacy groups.
  • Local pharmacies via "NOW!". A partnership for same-day delivery that turns rivals into fulfilment partners.

Fragility: moderate. Gematik's technical choices and insurers' rebates are policy variables outside Redcare's control. Gematik has repeatedly protected continuity (two CardLink extensions, with the option to extend again).

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

B+622nd of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: DocMorris is reviving. It helps the category and hurts at the margin. Local pharmacies are defending through politics and cooperatives, which hurts little today. Redcare's position is robust, but its lead in growth rate has reversed in 2026.

DocMorris:

  • Q3 2026 German Rx +78%; external revenue +22%; adjusted EBITDA break-even ahead of schedule; guidance raised twice.
  • Active customers 13.1m (pharmacy plus TeleClinic).
  • Marketing cut CHF 14m (310bp); customer acquisition cost "significantly and consistently reduced since Q4 2025".
  • AI-First savings of at least CHF 15m by FY27; about 100 posts cut; Ludwigshafen closed and logistics consolidated into automated Heerlen.
  • TeleClinic +48%.
  • Average order value €110 (a higher-Rx mix) against Redcare's €68.

The fee reform benefits it less (€3.7-5.2m against Redcare's €9.5-13.3m, Deutsche Bank).

Helps: a healthy number two grows awareness of online Rx, keeps the duopoly rational and gives the political lobby less of a single target.

Hurts: it has funded share-taking capacity, a telemedicine funnel and momentum (app downloads +27% month on month in September against Redcare's +21%; active-user rebound of 10% against 3%). Note, though, that DocMorris' active app users are still down 15% year on year against Redcare's +7%.

Valuation signal. Jefferies notes Redcare trades at 0.45x FY27 EV/sales against DocMorris' 0.57-0.59x. The market prices the turnaround optionality, not the scale lead.

Local pharmacies and wholesalers:

  • gesund.de (Phoenix and Sanacorp backed) offers CardLink transfers to about 5,000 pharmacies, plus PAYBACK.
  • White-label shops.
  • Lobbying for a ban, temperature rules and signature rules; litigation (IhreApotheken against Zava teleprescriptions).

So far these have had little effect: younger cohorts bypass white-label solutions.

Fragility: low over 18 months for the leadership position. Medium for the growth-rate premium against DocMorris, which is already gone in 2026. The model should assume Redcare's share of online Rx drifts slightly down from 67% while its absolute growth stays high.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?A701st of 11full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

A701st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Disruption is acting on almost every actor in the chain, and in nearly every case it shifts value towards scaled online dispensers. Redcare's position after disruption is strong. Its fragility sits in two nodes it does not control: the state and gematik.

  • Demand (patients). Digitisation of the script, apps, Health ID and higher co-payments make patients channel-agnostic for planned chronic care. Effect: favourable. The elderly are the friction.
  • Competition (local pharmacies). Loss of the paper-script and proximity advantage, staffing shortages, sub-3% EBIT margins and closures. Effect: favourable. Their response is political.
  • Competition (online peers). Consolidation: small players fail, DocMorris restructures and uses AI. Effect: favourable overall, with a capable number two.
  • Regulators and politicians. Moving from protecting incumbents to digitising the system (the e-Rx mandate, PoPP), while managing cost (rebates, co-payments). Effect: favourable now, a fragile node.
  • Wholesalers. Squeezed between direct manufacturer sourcing and online pharmacies; responding through gesund.de, white-label shops and a renegotiation of purchase conditions with online players. Effect: mixed. Redcare's terms are a moderate risk.
  • Manufacturers. Some resist cross-border direct supply. Direct-to-consumer models are not material in Europe. They are moving into retail media budgets, which is Redcare's opportunity.
  • Technology providers. Gematik, RISE and the Health ID providers carry implementation risk; the PoPP phases are on schedule so far.
  • Carriers and labour. Wage inflation drives automation and lockers.

Fragility. The position is fragile only where a policy actor changes the rules. A competitive or technological shock alone is unlikely to dislodge the leader within three years.

C-39Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?A621st of 11full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
3b

Industry structure

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

A851st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Redcare is the number one in its activity, by a wide margin:

  • Europe's largest dedicated online pharmacy: FY25 revenue €2,941m, against DocMorris' CHF 1.19bn external revenue (about 2.3x) and Apotea's roughly SEK 8.4bn.
  • The leader in German online Rx: 67% of online e-Rx redemptions (Deutsche Bank and the company). Its German Rx sales are about twice DocMorris'.
  • One of the top two players that hold up to 90% of German online OTC volume.
  • Leader in Swiss Rx mail-order through MediService.
  • The "clear market leader in five countries" by its own claim.

The non-obvious point. German ownership law forbids chains: one owner may run at most one main pharmacy plus three branches, and outside capital may not own pharmacies. So no domestic pharmacy business can approach Redcare's scale. On German Rx sales of about €745m in FY26, Redcare is very probably the largest single pharmacy business serving Germany. Yet it holds only about 1.3-1.4% of a €55bn Rx market. The relevant rank is first in online and first among single operators, inside a channel that is 1-2% of the market.

Distance from number two. DocMorris is the only scaled competitor, at roughly a third to a half of Redcare's German Rx size. It is closing the gap at the margin (Q3 2026 German Rx growth of 78% against Redcare's 56%, from a smaller base), but is not near parity. In active customers it has 12.9m (11.4m excluding TeleClinic) against Redcare's 14.7m.

Source conflict. The 67% online share and the "Redcare plus DocMorris = 80%" figure come from different definitions and sources. Treat 67% as Redcare's share of app/online e-Rx redemptions, and do not combine the two.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

A821st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The market has two tiers. Redcare's channel is extremely concentrated inside a total market that is atomised. That configuration favours the online leaders.

  • Online German Rx: the top two hold about 80% (Morgan Stanley); Redcare alone about 67%. The top five easily hold over 30%. The distribution is very steep: number one at about 67%, number two at roughly 15-25%, and a long tail now failing (Disapo and Sanicare insolvent in 2026).
  • Online German OTC: the top two hold up to 90% of volume (expert). dm, Rossmann and Amazon are marginal.
  • Total German pharmacy market: about 16,600 pharmacies, about 95% independently owned. The top five hold well under 30%, and chains are legally barred.
  • Sweden, the precedent: Apotea holds about 40-45% of online and 10.6% of the total. Apotek Hjärtat (ICA) holds 32.7% of the total. Online consolidated faster than offline.

Implication. Inside the online channel the structure is a near-monopoly leader plus one challenger. That supports rational pricing and shared investment in market development: the experts call the relationship "constructive". The offline incumbents are fragmented, can only lobby or join cooperatives (gesund.de), and cannot match bonuses, automation or national marketing. That is close to the ideal structure for a scaled online leader. The risk is regulatory, not structural.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

A781st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Online shares have been stable-to-rising for Redcare over three to five years. New entrants have taken negligible share.

Redcare against DocMorris. Redcare's three-year revenue CAGR is 22.4% against DocMorris' 2.5% (Jefferies). Redcare widened its lead through the 2024-26 e-Rx ramp; Deutsche Bank's April 2026 note is titled "the competitive gap widens". In 2026 DocMorris has started to grow Rx faster from a smaller base (Q2 +46% against Redcare's +58%; Q3 +78% against +56%). Since Redcare's absolute increase is still larger, relative share is now roughly stable rather than widening.

New entrants:

  • dm (online OTC since December 2024, about 2,500 products): "no operational impact", "failed to capture meaningful market share", despite triggering a 25% fall in Redcare's shares in December 2024.
  • Rossmann: not yet launched (planned for late 2026).
  • Amazon: no meaningful Rx share in ten years.
  • gesund.de: a cooperative platform for incumbents, not a new competitor.
  • Exits: Disapo and Sanicare.

Base rate. Experts expect dm to take at most 10-15% of online OTC in the long run. That is the largest plausible new-entrant share, and it sits in the lower-value category.

Stability judgement. Market shares are stable at the top. The pattern of share change in the last five years has been consolidation towards the two leaders. The one source of instability is DocMorris' turnaround.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

A801st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Yes on both counts, and Redcare is the main beneficiary.

Why share consolidates (mechanisms with evidence):

  1. Fixed-cost scale. Automation (Sevenum: 35m-plus parcels a year, doubling) and national brand marketing are fixed costs. Smaller players "fail due to extreme infrastructure investment burdens" (Disapo, Sanicare). Losing volume with high fixed costs turns automated logistics into traps for regional players.
  2. Purchasing. More than €1bn of sales gives "immense wholesale bargaining leverage" (expert). Redcare's procurement spend is €2.44bn.
  3. Customer acquisition efficiency. National awareness and app scale lower acquisition cost per customer. Redcare is "the dominant advertising force in Germany".
  4. Regulatory and technical compliance. Gematik/CardLink certification, pharmacists, storage and signature rules raise the minimum efficient scale.
  5. The Swedish precedent. Online consolidated to Apotea (40-45% online share), the most automated and lowest-cost player, not the largest legacy pharmacy.

Most advantaged, not just largest. Redcare holds the best cost position (Pilsen wages plus Sevenum automation) and the best delivery speed (up to one day faster than DocMorris, per expert). It is "one to two years ahead" in marketplace and retail media capability. DocMorris has the advantage in telemedicine (TeleClinic) and is moving fast on AI.

Wisdom Library check. "Scale needs a bounded market" is satisfied. The throughput unit is Rx packs or orders. The gap to the nearest rival is about 2x. Minimum efficient scale is high (automation plus certification). The market is bounded by national regulation and language.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C+663rd of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The answer differs by segment.

German Rx: rational, margin-first and implicitly cooperative today, inside a regulated fixed price.

  • Prices cannot fall when volume falls; the state sets them.
  • Competition takes the form of bonuses, co-payment cover and marketing.
  • Both leaders are cutting marketing. Experts describe them as "shifting focus from growth to profitability", "adopting more moderate pricing and marketing strategies" and sharing "a constructive relationship aimed at expanding the overall online pharmacy penetration rate".
  • DocMorris, after years of losses and capital raises, is now margin-first: AI-First savings, warehouse consolidation, break-even. Deutsche Bank says it is "actively managing OTC sales to focus on high-margin Rx conversion rather than unprofitable discounting".

German OTC and beauty: volume-first and price-transparent.

  • Prices fall when demand softens. Late 2025 OTC weakness led to promotional bonus campaigns.
  • Redcare itself prices up to 10% below competitors on up to half its OTC range.
  • dm and Rossmann are low-price retailers by model.
  • Sweden shows the endgame: online discounters forced store chains to cut beauty prices (Inderes, June 2026).

Local pharmacies. They compete through lobbying, legal action and cooperative platforms, not price. They are financially unable to offer bonuses even where permitted.

Implication. The profit pool that matters (Rx) has a benign structure. The pool that is competitive (OTC) is where Redcare's scale lets it be the price-setter, by sharing scale economies with customers.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

C-505th of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The attitude is moderately stable now, but it has a history of instability. The most likely "shark" is a recapitalised DocMorris, or Redcare itself.

History over 18 months to 3 years.

  • 2023-25 was a promotional land-grab by both leaders as e-Rx became mandatory. A former DocMorris chief executive says a "heavy marketing war was essential", with coupons of up to €20 for first app-based redemptions.
  • Redcare stepped up marketing aggressively in late 2024 and ran heavy bonus campaigns in Q4 2025. Its margin collapsed to 1.6% that quarter, and the March 2026 reset followed.
  • DocMorris was capital-starved: 98% of value destroyed (activists' claim), CHF 200m raised, 32.6% short interest. That forced it into restraint.

So the current calm comes from both players being forced into margin discipline: Redcare by credibility, DocMorris by its balance sheet. It was not chosen out of strength.

What could change it over the next 12-18 months.

  1. DocMorris has refinanced, reached break-even and raised guidance. Jefferies notes that its improved maturity profile "allows the Swiss peer to refocus on growth, potentially increasing competitive marketing pressure".
  2. The 2027 co-payment rise is a natural trigger for co-payment cover competition.
  3. Rossmann (deep pockets, private, unpredictable) launching in OTC.
  4. A new Redcare chief executive from marketplaces, where land-grabs are the norm, could choose to press the advantage.

Shark-attack test. If Redcare showed weakness, for example a PoPP transition stumble in 2027-28 or a Sevenum commissioning problem, DocMorris has both the motive (it is closing a 2x gap) and now the means. Advertising law limits the form of attack (Karlsruhe voucher ruling).

Skew: this is the main competitive tail. Its cost would show up in marketing ratio and bonus cost, not in Rx prices.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

A741st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Very supportive. A fixed-price, state-indexed profit pool per pack is migrating from a fragmented, legally chain-barred incumbent base to an online duopoly. That duopoly is protected by certification, scale and a legal asymmetry: foreign mail-order may pay bonuses, domestic pharmacies cannot. The fragility is mostly political, and secondarily competitive.

Support, ranked by importance:

  1. Fixed Rx prices, so no price competition on product.
  2. A rising fixed fee per pack (Fixum).
  3. Fragmented, financially weak incumbents who cannot scale (ownership law) and cannot pay bonuses.
  4. An online duopoly with 80% share and rational behaviour.
  5. High minimum efficient scale, which bars entrants (insolvencies).
  6. Supplier wholesale terms capped by statute, so rivals cannot buy cheaper.
  7. Demand that is inelastic and recurring (chronic, 90% repeat).

Fragility, ranked:

  1. Politics. Bonus restrictions, rebate increases or, in the extreme, a mail-order ban. Wisdom Library: "state-granted moats are absolute and fragile". Probability is low and rising slowly with online share. Magnitude is high.
  2. Duopoly behaviour. A bonus war in 2027 is the medium-probability, moderate-magnitude risk.
  3. Technology transition. PoPP/Health ID in 2027-28; low-to-medium probability, moderate magnitude, mitigated by gematik's flexibility.
  4. OTC entrants. High probability of noise, low magnitude.

Peer reference. CVS (UK vets) shows how a regulator (the CMA) can overhang a consolidating industry for two years, then release it with benign remedies. Germany's political overhang is the equivalent risk here, not yet a live investigation.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?A701st of 11full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

A701st of 11

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The main substitute is the local pharmacy. E-Rx is eroding its advantages for chronic, planned demand and preserving them for acute, advisory demand. The substitution threat is falling for the profit pool that matters.

For chronic Rx. The substitute (the local pharmacy) offers immediacy, personal advice and trust. Redcare offers home delivery, a bonus or co-payment offset (illegal for local pharmacies), app convenience and fewer doctor trips (six-month cycles). E-Rx made the two channels equivalent at the point of prescription; paper used to favour the local pharmacy physically. Trust and demographics remain the substitute's defence: Germany is "a very old market where trust and physical presence remain predominant forces" (expert). Closures (below 16,600) shrink the substitute.

For acute Rx. Next-day delivery loses to local immediacy. The "NOW!" same-day partnership is a partial bridge. Concede this pool.

For OTC and beauty. Drugstores, supermarkets, Amazon and discounters are close substitutes. The threat is high and stable, and price comparison makes it visible.

Emerging alternatives. Telemedicine plus pharmacy bundles (DocMorris) substitute for Redcare's funnel, not for its product. Manufacturer direct-to-consumer is minimal in Europe. Hybrid local delivery through quick commerce is at the margin.

Change. Positive for Rx. The digital ID infrastructure (PoPP, Health ID) and higher co-payments both push planned chronic demand online. The ceiling debate is the real substitute question: experts see 10% medium-term against Sweden's 12% online Rx share, against a 20-25% five-year view from another expert. Any of these is many multiples of today's 1-2%.

4a

Business quality

A721st of 118 answers
Judgement72C-4172C-4280C-4382C-4475C-4538C-4666C-4780
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

A721st of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Redcare's advantage is real, measurable and widening in the near term:

  • about 2x the scale of the only scaled rival;
  • the lowest cost per order coming in 2027, from automation and Czech wages;
  • a legal right shared only with other foreign mail-order pharmacies to pay patients bonuses that incumbents cannot;
  • sticky chronic cohorts;
  • a platform lead in retail media and marketplace.

Mechanical forces (cohort layering, fixed-cost absorption, fee step-ups and the end of the capex cycle) do most of the work, which makes improvement forecastable.

The weaknesses:

  • No pricing power on Rx, and deliberately little on OTC.
  • No network effect.
  • A state-granted core. The most powerful advantage can be legislated away.
  • DocMorris holds the better telemedicine funnel and has closed the cost gap.

For the model: the advantage justifies stable-to-slightly-falling share of online Rx, rising gross profit per Rx pack (Fixum), falling cost per order from 2027, and part of the gains recycled to customers from late 2027.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

A721st of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Redcare holds four real advantages and one temporary one. The strongest is a combination of scale-based cost and a regulatory asymmetry. No single customer moat would hold on its own.

  1. Scale-based cost and acquisition advantage. Strong; widening into 2027. - About 2-2.3x the revenue of the number two. - Sevenum (35m-plus parcels a year, doubling capacity, 70% lower labour cost per order from 2027) plus Pilsen at Czech wages. - Procurement of €2.44bn, with about 70% bought direct from manufacturers. - National brand and app scale lower the cost of acquiring each customer. - "Scale needs a bounded market" holds: Rx packs are the throughput unit, the gap to the number two is about 2x, minimum efficient scale is high and the market is bounded by national regulation. - It narrows only if DocMorris' Heerlen automation and AI-First savings close the cost-per-order gap. Experts already call fulfilment "near-parity".
  2. Regulatory or political asymmetry. Very strong but fragile; stable. - As an EU-domiciled mail-order pharmacy (Dutch), Redcare may pay Rx bonuses and cover co-payments. German local pharmacies may not (BGH, July 2025). - German ownership law stops domestic chains forming, so no domestic rival can match its scale. - Licensing, pharmacist staffing and certified storage bar generalists such as Amazon. - Wisdom Library: "state-granted moats are absolute and fragile". - It is shared with DocMorris, so it protects the duopoly against incumbents, not Redcare against DocMorris.
  3. Customer: habit and app lock-in among chronic patients. Medium; widening. - 90% repeat orders, e-Rx NPS of 77-80, 2025 Rx cohorts at 2x the gross profit of non-Rx cohorts, CardLink in-app only, six-month repeat cycles. - Switching costs are low in principle (any pharmacy can fill an e-Rx), but habit and app setup create inertia. - It deepens as Health ID makes the app the default redemption route.
  4. Knowledge, culture and platform: marketplace, retail media and pricing analytics. Medium; widening under the new management. - Experts put the platform "one to two years ahead of DocMorris" in marketplace capability. - It offers daily retail media analytics that suppliers prefer, and AI pricing tested in Austria. - Delivery is up to a day faster than DocMorris thanks to centralised logistics.
  5. Network effects. None material. The marketplace is under 5% of sales, and a pharmacy is a pipeline, not a platform. Do not credit one.

Temporary, not a moat: the CardLink first-mover position. The technology is shared and will be superseded by PoPP. "The early-mover window is not a moat."

Net: moat strength is high against incumbents and new entrants, and moderate against DocMorris. Widening through 2027 on automation and monetisation; vulnerable to a policy change.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

A801st of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Redcare is the largest operator in its channel by a factor of about two, and by orders of magnitude larger than any German pharmacy. That scale confers lower cost per order, cheaper acquisition, retail media pricing power with suppliers, and the ability to fund regulatory fights and bonuses.

Comparison:

Actor Scale
Redcare FY25 revenue €2,941m; 14.7m active customers; 12.3m orders in Q2 2026 (12.6m in Q1); German Rx about €745m in FY26
DocMorris CHF 1.19bn external revenue; 12.9m customers (11.4m excluding TeleClinic); German Rx roughly half of Redcare's
Apotea over 3m customers; about SEK 8.4bn revenue
Average German pharmacy about €3-4m revenue (my estimate: market value divided by about 16,600 pharmacies)
Galenica 384 Swiss pharmacies; CHF 756.9m of Products and Care sales in H1 2026

Logistics:

  • Sevenum handles more than 35m parcels a year, with capacity doubling from Q1 2027.
  • Pilsen adds 15m orders a year; Settala (Italy) is also in the network.
  • "Sufficient fulfilment runway for the next five years", with no major capacity investment needed.

Procurement: €2.44bn of spend across more than 400 suppliers.

What scale confers, with evidence:

  1. Unit cost. Automation and wage arbitrage that sub-scale players cannot fund (the insolvencies).
  2. Acquisition efficiency. Redcare's marketing ratio fell to 5.1% while it remained "the dominant advertising force in Germany".
  3. Supplier economics. Direct sourcing and volume discounts, and a retail media audience that 150 suppliers pay to reach.
  4. Bonus capacity and resilience. The ability to absorb promotional cost and regulatory change.
  5. A larger absolute fee tailwind. €9.5-13.3m against DocMorris' €3.7-5.2m in 2027.

What scale does not confer:

  • Rx pricing (fixed by the state).
  • A network effect.
  • Any immunity from political attention, which scale actually increases.

Headcount is not given in the sources.

C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?A821st of 11full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

A751st of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Inferiority is disproved for chronic Rx and OTC customers who are digitally capable. Redcare probably leads its online competitor and beats local pharmacies on value. It is inferior for acute needs and for elderly, advice-seeking patients, and it concedes those segments.

Evidence against inferiority (the test is to disprove inferiority):

  • Satisfaction. e-Rx NPS 77 (Q2 2026), 80 (August 2026, +6 points year on year), the highest since launch. Overall NPS 74.
  • Behaviour. Repeat orders 90%, against DocMorris' 76% on trailing data. Return rate 0.7%.
  • Price and value. Rx price is fixed, but Redcare pays bonuses and co-payment offsets that local pharmacies legally cannot. OTC prices up to 10% below rivals. An assortment of more than 500,000 products, against dm's 2,500.
  • Convenience. Home delivery and app scanning; six-month cycles; delivery up to a day faster than DocMorris (expert); "NOW!" same-day where needed.
  • Experts. Redcare's "superior operational execution and centralized logistics yield higher customer satisfaction than DocMorris".

Where it is inferior:

  • Immediacy for acute needs.
  • Personal advice, for older patients who prefer face-to-face contact.
  • Onboarding friction for non-tech-savvy patients (doctor, card and app steps).
  • Low awareness: only 35% know CardLink.

Against DocMorris specifically. DocMorris offers an integrated telemedicine-to-pharmacy journey (TeleClinic) and an AI assistant used by a third of its app users. For patients without a regular doctor that is a superior proposition. Redcare does not lead on that dimension.

Direction: improving. PoPP/Health ID removes the remaining onboarding friction (no SMS or PIN), and higher co-payments raise the value of bonuses.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F3810th of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Pricing power is low and indirect. On Rx the state sets the price, and the "price rise" (Fixum) is granted, not taken. On OTC Redcare deliberately prices below the market and refuses to raise prices.

The demonstrable levers are three:

  1. Lowering give-backs without losing volume. Marketing intensity fell 210bp while German Rx growth accelerated, so the "negative price" of acquisition fell. This is real evidence of improved commercial power.
  2. Mix and price optimisation by algorithm. AI pricing added about 2 percentage points to German non-Rx growth. The German non-Rx gross margin rose more than 2 points quarter on quarter in Q2 2026.
  3. Pricing to suppliers. Retail media rates and marketplace commission are the one place where Redcare sets prices with some freedom. Suppliers prefer its retail media. There is no figure, as the line is unsegmented.

Against pricing power:

  • OTC comparison sites (Medizinfuchs) automatically route baskets to the cheapest pharmacy.
  • Management will not raise OTC prices, to protect trust.
  • Late-2025 OTC weakness required promotions.
  • Swiss prices are being cut by the regulator.

Evidence of application: the gross margin did not recover as Rx mix rose (22.1% in Q2 2026 against 23.8% in Q2 2025). It improved 110bp quarter on quarter from Q1, on retail media normalisation and pricing tools.

Wisdom Library framing. "Pricing power is rare in a regulated industry, so the market pays for it." Redcare's earnings gains come from volume and fixed costs, not price. The sector digest says the same: "an online pharmacy cannot raise the price of a German prescription medicine".

Model implication. Hold OTC price and mix flat to slightly positive. Model Rx gross profit per pack from Fixum minus rebate. Treat the co-payment cover cost as a negative price risk.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

A661st of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Yes, de facto and substantially, though not as an explicit doctrine in the "Costco" sense. Redcare passes scale economies back through four routes:

  • OTC prices up to 10% below rivals, "supported by automated cost efficiencies";
  • Rx cash bonuses and co-payment offsets;
  • assortment breadth and free or fast delivery;
  • service investments (app, pharmacist consultation, NOW!).

Management says it competes "primarily on service quality rather than aggressive price wars", and refuses OTC price rises that would damage trust. "Because We Care" is the culture banner.

The judgement that matters. Sharing scale economies is what keeps Redcare's OTC share against dm and Rossmann. It is also what has kept its margin at 2-3.5% for years. The question for the model is how much of the 2027 gains will be shared rather than retained:

  • the Fixum gain of €9.5-13.3m;
  • Sevenum, about €10m;
  • marketing leverage.

My judgement: most of the Fixum and automation gains will be retained in 2027. Redcare is the only player able to fund co-payment cover at scale. Both leaders are in margin mode. The new management is explicitly EPS-focused. A meaningful share of the gains (perhaps a third) is likely to be recycled into co-payment cover and OTC price as competition responds in late 2027 and 2028.

That recycling is what turns the "scale economies shared" flywheel from a defence into share gains. It is also why the 5% mid-term margin, rather than 8%, is the realistic mid-term ceiling.

Peer reference. Apotea shares savings through price and is the Swedish price leader. It still earns a 4-6% EBIT margin at maturity.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

A801st of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Strongly. Most of the improvement Redcare will report over the next 18 months to three years is mechanical: it needs execution, not heroics. That makes the base case unusually forecastable for a growth company.

The mechanical forces:

  1. Cohort layering. Each year's Rx cohort keeps ordering (90% repeat; 4.5x loyalty for e-Rx cohorts at DocMorris), so gross profit from past cohorts arrives with no new marketing. Marketing as a share of revenue therefore falls automatically if acquisition spend is held flat.
  2. Fixed-cost absorption. Automated hubs, IT and G&A grow far more slowly than orders. Incremental EBITDA margin was about 8% in Q2 2026 (revenue +€144m, adjusted EBITDA +€11.6m year on year). Consensus implies about 9% for FY27 and about 10.5% for FY28.
  3. The regulated fee step-ups. €9.00 in July 2026 and €9.50 in January 2027.
  4. Demographics. An ageing population means more chronic packs per patient.
  5. Erosion of local pharmacies. Closures, staffing shortages and sub-3% margins hand over patients.
  6. Digital ID adoption. Health ID and the EU wallet will lower onboarding friction over time.
  7. The capex cycle ending. Capex falls from about 4% of revenue (FY25) to below 2% from FY27, so free cash flow rises mechanically.
  8. The generational shift. Digital natives are ageing into chronic therapy.

Where dynamism is still required:

  • commissioning Sevenum without a 2021-style disruption;
  • winning the co-payment cover contest rationally;
  • building retail media and marketplace;
  • converting older patients through PoPP.

Against peers. DocMorris has the same mechanical forces at half the scale, which is why it is now reaching break-even. Local pharmacies have the opposite forces acting on them.

Wisdom Library. This is a "flywheel" (Gold) rather than a "treadmill", provided the marketing ratio stays near 5%. A rising marketing ratio would be the sign that the flywheel had become a treadmill.

Company - Management

C+603rd of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The management system is in transition from a capable pharmacy operator team to a finance-and-platform team, under a founder-chair.

Proven:

  • delivery of German Rx leadership, MediService, automation, International break-even and marketing discipline;
  • a credible, conservative finance director who rebuilt guidance trust;
  • prudent governance scaffolding;
  • clean insider behaviour.

Unproven:

  • the new chief executive (three weeks in post);
  • the leadership bench (vacant commercial director post);
  • the ability to navigate 2027 co-payment competition and PoPP politics without pharmacy expertise at the top;
  • the conversion of a decade of growth into returns on capital and buybacks.

Weak:

  • pay-for-performance in 2025;
  • warehouse retention;
  • disclosure of the variables that would expose bought growth (co-payment spend, cohort churn).

For the model: management quality supports execution of the mechanical plan (Sevenum, marketing about 5%, Fixum capture) with medium-to-high confidence. It does not yet support crediting upside from platform monetisation beyond a modest contribution until the CMD and 2027 results show it.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

A701st of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: The ambition is large and concrete, and higher than peers': to be the default digital pharmacy for German chronic patients and Europe's leading online pharmacy, converting scale into a cash compounder.

The stated ambition:

  • "strengthen European online pharmacy leadership";
  • "capture massive online prescription market growth";
  • Rx focus for customer lifetime value;
  • an above-8% long-term EBITDA margin "aligning with Apotea";
  • 5% by FY28;
  • a "predictable cash flow compounder";
  • under the new chief executive, AI and marketplace monetisation.

The ambition was tempered in March 2026:

  • the mid-term margin target went from 8% to 5%;
  • the finance director pushed back on "hockey-stick" penetration and guided to "gradual and predictable" increases.

That tempering is credibility management, not shrinkage of vision.

Relative to peers:

  • DocMorris has been in survival and turnaround mode (break-even, AI-First cost cuts).
  • Apotea targets doubling revenue in four to five years (15-19% CAGR) with a 7-8% EBIT margin. That is comparable growth ambition, at a higher margin ambition.
  • Galenica defends.

Redcare is the only player with a stated plan to dominate a €55bn market's channel shift.

Force markers:

  • growth as a priority (yes);
  • outcompeting (yes: 10% lower OTC prices, bonuses, marketing dominance);
  • innovating (CardLink pioneer, the first new telematics-infrastructure access, AI pricing);
  • pushing (lobbying for PoPP and EU e-Rx).
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

B+682nd of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities?

Judgement: The strategy is right and coherent, with a moderate degree of boldness. It widens the moat (cost leadership, Rx cohorts, monetisation) and prioritises growth in the profitable pool. It is dynamic in some environments but not in all: it is weak if politics turn and thin in telemedicine.

Right priorities:

  1. Rx focus. It is the inelastic, regulated, recurring pool. The cohorts are worth 2x the gross profit of non-Rx cohorts, and the Rx funnel cross-sells OTC (40.8% mixed orders).
  2. Cost leadership (Sevenum automation plus Pilsen) for absolute unit-cost advantage.
  3. App-first redemption, matching CardLink and PoPP.
  4. Monetisation of traffic (retail media, marketplace, own brands).
  5. Marketing discipline and EPS focus.
  6. The Swiss joint venture as a profitable, partner-protected option on liberalisation.

Bold elements:

  • The 2025 Rx bonus launch ahead of competitors.
  • The €100m automation investment through a downturn in the share price ("capacity to suffer").
  • The appointment of a marketplace chief executive.

Gaps:

  1. No owned telemedicine or prescriber funnel, where DocMorris' TeleClinic is a structural route to new Rx patients.
  2. No explicit political or regulatory strategy beyond advocacy (PoPP, EU e-Rx). Given state-granted profits, a stakeholder strategy (for example partnerships with local pharmacies through NOW!, insurer partnerships) is under-developed in the disclosures.
  3. France unresolved.

Dynamism across environments:

  • Strong in a benign duopoly.
  • Adequate in a bonus war (Redcare has the deepest pockets).
  • Weak in a regulatory reversal: no Plan B for a bonus ban beyond service quality.

Wisdom Library. "Name the marathon, then find the moat." The marathon is cost per Rx pack and patient lifetime value, and the strategy names it.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

B+642nd of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Well resourced physically and financially; under-resourced in people and some systems. The physical network is built "yesterday for resilience". The organisation is not yet.

Well resourced:

  • Logistics. Sevenum (€100m automation, doubling capacity, commissioning Q1 2027), Pilsen (15m orders) and Settala. Capacity for five years of growth with no major new capex.
  • Financing. A €300m convertible to 2032 at 1.75%, €119m of cash, no maturities until 2030.
  • Technology. CardLink pioneer; the first to use new telematics-infrastructure access; AI pricing; an app with 76% mobile traffic.
  • Supplier relationships. €252m of trade payables, direct manufacturer deals, 150-plus retail media clients.
  • Governance scaffolding. Internal audit (2025), Speak Up line (2024), a quality assurance department, ISO 14001 targeted for Sevenum.

Under-resourced:

  • People. Warehouse turnover of 39.1%; a vacant commercial director post; leadership churn; recruitment constraints flagged.
  • Systems. Retail media systems "failed to scale fast enough". The Pilsen transition caused Austrian supply disruption in Q1 2026.
  • Working capital buffers. Inventory was raised to protect service, which shows deliberate resilience-building at a cash cost.
  • A single critical hub. Sevenum handles all Rx. The Czech law against cross-border Rx shipments means Rx cannot move to Pilsen. Power and IT outages (flagged risks) or commissioning problems at Sevenum therefore hit Rx directly.

Wisdom Library. "Capacity to suffer shows up in the accounts". Redcare front-loaded €100m of automation and €118m of FY25 capex (company figure) through a share-price collapse. Traction was visible (Rx growth) and long-term owners (Köhler, Galenica) were present, so the test is met.

Mid-term record (3-5 years):

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

A621st of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Governance is prudent and improving, with well-built scaffolding: a single share class, an independent board, internal audit, long-term-only pay with clawback, and no executive selling. Shareholder-first behaviour is good but not exemplary. Pay has been disconnected from shareholder outcomes. The convertible financing protected the company at shareholders' potential expense. Returns policy is appropriately absent.

Evidence for shareholders first and prudence:

  • One share, one vote; no voting restrictions. Contrast DocMorris' 3% nominee cap and Apotea's tenth-vote shares.
  • The supervisory board is 80% independent (four of five).
  • Audit, remuneration and nomination committees.
  • Unqualified audits (Forvis Mazars).
  • Internal audit established in 2025, curing a code breach.
  • Speak Up line: 14 reports in its first quarter.
  • The founders' 25-member voting pool was dissolved in May 2023.
  • No related-party conflicts approved in 2025. One small 2025 transaction: €241,660 of carbon credits bought from a firm managed by a former director, at market terms.
  • Pay: long-term incentives only, with three-year vesting on revenue plus margin, 20% ESG and Dutch clawback. The managing board option cap is 130,000 shares (about 0.6% of the share count).
  • Zero open-market selling by the managing board over 12 months, despite volatility. That places it in roughly the top 5% of European mid-caps.
  • Founder-chair Köhler holds 8.3%: aligned capital at the top.
  • Buybacks rejected until net profitability (prudent).
  • Scaffolding built ahead of need (internal audit, quality assurance, Speak Up).

Evidence against:

  • Pay versus outcomes. CEO total pay rose 21% to €1,129,650 in 2025 while market capitalisation fell 52%. The base salary was flat at €500,004, so the rise came from equity and long-term awards.
  • Shareholding requirement. The CEO had not met the 125%-of-salary requirement by late 2025, and there is no evidence of insider buying by the new CEO.
  • Adjusted measures exclude ESOP cost, the measure on which vesting partly depends.
  • The convertible carries future dilution. The repurchase of the 2028 converts at an elevated finance cost was prudent for refinancing risk but costly.
  • Disclosure gaps remain (cohort churn, co-payment spend).

Why. The founder-chair and strategic holders give long-term ownership. The Dutch governance code and the post-2023 professionalisation drive the scaffolding. Pay design follows growth-company norms.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

C+563rd of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: It is a competent operator-allocator with one excellent deal (MediService), one sound large project (Sevenum, return yet to be proven), one failure (GoPuls), and refinancing that was defensive rather than value-creating. It is not a repurchaser, and cannot yet be one.

Record:

  1. MediService 51% joint venture (May 2023). Excellent. Swiss Rx scale (€307m in 2023, €566m in FY25), profitable and cash-generative, partner-endorsed, and an option on Swiss liberalisation. Gross margin diluted, but returns on the capital used are high.
  2. Sevenum automation (€100m) and Pilsen. Sound and timely. The return depends on volume, which is arriving. Deutsche Bank's +50bp of EBITDA and €10m of efficiencies in 2027 on about €100m invested imply a pre-tax return of about 10%, plus capacity for growth.
  3. Rx bonus and marketing investment in 2024-26. High return on cohort evidence (2x gross profit), though it was over-spent in late 2024 and Q4 2025.
  4. Smaller acquisitions. smartpatient and MedApp (2021): unclear. GoPuls: failed, written down, €13m contingent consideration released. AustPharma (€0.3m): tactical.
  5. Financing. The €300m 2032 convertible at 1.75% plus 110% redemption, and the repurchase of €157.9m of 2028 converts. This removed refinancing risk but raised finance expense and kept dilution potential.
  6. Returns to shareholders. None. Buybacks are ruled out until consistent profitability; no dividend.

Judgement. The allocation in 2023-26 was sensible and the big bets were right: Rx, Switzerland, automation. Whether this team becomes an enlightened allocator will be decided in 2027-29. From FY27 free cash flow turns positive and the questions will be telemedicine M&A, International, buybacks and convertible management.

Financial profile

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

C+564th of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Economic strength today is weak in level and strong in direction:

  • EBITDA margins of 2-3.5%;
  • negative reported EBIT and EPS until FY27;
  • negative ROIC (-4.2%);
  • negative free cash flow in FY25 and FY26;
  • a noisy earnings base below EBITDA (convertible accounting, tax, minorities, ESOP).

The incremental economics, though, are very strong:

  • 8-10% incremental EBITDA margins;
  • very high returns on new capital, because the physical network is built;
  • capex falling below 2% of revenue;
  • a regulated per-pack fee rising 13% net;
  • a mechanical cohort flywheel.

That combination produces steep, leverage-driven EPS growth from FY27. It has a wide cone because of high operating and inflection-year financial leverage.

The Consumer should model:

  • gross profit per German Rx pack explicitly;
  • incremental EBITDA margins of 8-11%;
  • D&A rising with Sevenum;
  • net finance cost of about €25-30m;
  • a low but rising tax rate (loss carryforwards to be confirmed);
  • a 49% MediService minority leak;
  • working capital absorbing 3-5% of incremental revenue.

The quality of earnings will improve markedly once free cash flow turns positive in FY27.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

C605th of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Redcare is generating no positive EPS yet. Consensus reported EPS was -€1.87 in FY25. The FY26 consensus is -€0.21 (median; the mean is -€0.28). Positive EPS arrives in FY27 at €1.32 and FY28 at €2.85 on adjusted brokers' basis, on revenue growth of 17.5%, 12.7% and 10.8%.

This EPS growth is leverage, not compounding. Small changes in EBITDA create large percentage changes in EPS:

  • About €70-85m of D&A and about €25-30m of net finance cost sit between EBITDA and pre-tax profit (consensus FY27: EBITDA €138m, EBIT €53m, PBT €28m, net income €22-27m).
  • Each €10m of EBITDA is worth about €0.35-0.38 of EPS (at about 25% tax and 20.1m shares). That is about 28% of the FY27 consensus EPS.

Brokers' dispersion confirms it. FY27 EPS ranges from €0.84 (Jefferies) to €2.04 (Deutsche Bank) on near-identical EBITDA (€138-139m). The disagreement sits below EBITDA (D&A, interest, tax) as much as in operations.

Durability. The EPS path is durable if three things hold:

  1. German Rx gross profit per pack and volume. These are regulated and recurring: high durability.
  2. Marketing near 5%. Medium durability; competitive.
  3. The OTC gross margin. Medium-low durability.

The quality of the revenue growth is improving: a larger share of it is regulated, recurring Rx. Revenue growth itself is decelerating from 24% (FY25) to the low-to-mid teens. That is fine for EPS, because the growth that remains carries an incremental margin of 8-10%, against a group margin of 3%.

Peer contrast:

  • Apotea: EPS growth of 26%, 25% and 19% (consensus FY26-28), on 12% revenue growth.
  • CVS: 11%, 9% and 8%.
  • Galenica: 3%, 10% and 4%.

Redcare's percentage EPS growth will dwarf all of these from FY28 (+115% in the peer table) because the base is near zero.

Cone: very wide in percentage terms. The FY27 EPS cone runs from about €0.5 to €2.0 on plausible operating outcomes.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

A721st of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The scope to deploy operating spend at high returns is large: Rx patient acquisition, retail media and marketplace, International scaling. The need for physical capital is small: capacity is in place for five years and capex runs below 2% of revenue. That combination of a long reinvestment runway with low capital intensity is the profile of a compounder, and it is at an early stage.

Where the money goes, and the evidence on returns:

  1. German Rx patient acquisition (marketing, bonuses, co-payment cover). - 2025 cohorts generated about 2x the gross profit of non-Rx cohorts and about 4x the revenue after six quarters. - Repeat rate is 90%. - Marketing is 5% of revenue, and its efficiency is rising. - The pool of unconverted chronic patients is tens of millions (active German Rx customers are only 1.73m). - The return is high so long as cohort retention holds. That is undisclosed, and is the main data gap.
  2. Fixed infrastructure already built. Sevenum and Pilsen absorb incremental orders at low marginal cost. Incremental return on capital for the next several hundred million euros of Rx revenue is very high: the working capital need is about 3-5% of revenue and little capex is needed.
  3. Retail media and marketplace. These are capital-light, high-margin and built on existing traffic.
  4. International. France is sub-scale. A partial return is still to be earned there.
  5. Optional: telemedicine (an acquisition or partnership) and EU cross-border prescriptions from 2029.

Constraints:

  • Advertising law limits the form of bonuses.
  • Political tolerance limits the size of bonuses.
  • OTC is saturated.

Implication for the multiple.

  • A business that can grow gross profit at about 15-20% a year for five or more years with low capital intensity deserves a premium to grocery-style e-retail.
  • The market currently pays 30.1x 24-month forward P/E, the 13th to 16th percentile of the stock's own history; about 13.4x FY27 EV/EBITDA and 10.2x FY28; 0.48x FY27 EV/sales.
  • Peers: Apotea at 23x P/E with a mature 4-6% EBIT margin; DocMorris at 0.57-0.59x FY27 EV/sales.

My judgement. On a 5% EBITDA margin by FY28-29 and a long runway, the fair multiple sits towards the upper half of Redcare's own range. Valuation work belongs to later sets; this note fixes only the direction.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

D526th of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Reported returns are negative today and will become respectable only from FY28 on consensus. Returns on incremental capital, however, are already very high. The gap between the two is the investment case.

Reported:

  • ROIC was -4.2% in FY25 (Jefferies), against Apotea's +12%. EBIT (adjusted, FactSet) was negative every year from FY19 to FY25 (FY25 -€23.8m, -0.8%).
  • ROE and ROCE are negative.

Capital employed. About €0.7-0.75bn: shareholders' equity of €464m (consensus FY26) plus FactSet net debt of €236m (including leases) plus minorities.

Forward, my arithmetic on consensus:

Year Consensus EBIT Post-tax at about 25% Approximate post-tax ROCE
FY27 €53m about €40m about 5%
FY28 €96m about €72m about 9-10%
FY29 (one broker) €183m about €137m about 17-18%

Return on tangible capital is materially higher. Goodwill and intangibles from MediService, the historical acquisitions and capitalised development are a large part of equity; the amount is not given in the sources. Dutch law restricts distributions until capitalised development is amortised, which signals that the capitalised amount is material.

Incremental return. From FY26 to FY28, consensus adds about €75m of EBIT on about €857m of extra revenue. That needs about €30-45m of working capital (3.5-5% of revenue) and maintenance capex. Physical expansion is already done (€100m Sevenum, Pilsen). Post-tax incremental ROIC on that tranche is therefore well above 50%. This is my calculation and depends on working capital staying near current intensity.

Interpretation. The legacy capital base (acquisition goodwill, the earlier Sevenum build, years of losses) depresses reported returns. New capital is earning very high returns. The model should show ROCE rising steeply as EBIT scales on a nearly flat capital base.

Peers: Apotea ROIC 12%; CVS ROCE 16.4% (hurdle 12%); Galenica in the high single digits.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

D507th of 11

Judgement: Historically, conversion is uninformative because net income was negative. Free cash flow was positive only in FY23 and has been consumed by the capex peak and by working capital since. Consensus shows free cash flow broadly matching net income from FY27 (1.1-1.3x). That is credible only if Rx-driven working capital growth stays modest. That is the cash flow line to watch.

Year FactSet free cash flow Company free cash flow
FY23 €51.8m €8.2m, the first positive
FY24 €11.4m not given
FY25 -€56.3m -€85.2m

Consensus:

  • Free cash flow: -€35m (FY26), +€28m (FY27), +€64m (FY28), +€151m (FY29).
  • Operating cash flow: €63.5m, €105m, €142m, €220m.
  • Capex: €89m, €71m, €76m, €90m.

Working capital behaviour, the critical judgement.

  • The model is supplier-funded. Payables of €252.2m (33 days) against inventory of €213.3m and receivables of €152.8m at H1 2026.
  • Three forces push working capital up: 1. German Rx receivables. Insurers pay after more than a month, while OTC is cash at checkout. Each additional €100m of German Rx revenue adds roughly €8-12m of receivables, on my assumption of 30-45 days. 2. Inventory. Up 32% year on year to €213m, including a temporary €10m buffer for the Pilsen ramp. 3. A possible reversal of stretched payables if wholesale terms tighten.
  • Measured trend. FactSet working capital (receivables plus inventory minus payables) was €35m (Q2 2025), €90.5m (Q4 2025) and €114m (Q2 2026), from 1.3% to 3.5% of trailing revenue. The company's own definition gives €85.5m (2.66%) at H1 2026. Definitions differ.

Other items:

  • Leases of €77.4m are not deducted in the FactSet free cash flow definition.
  • Dividends to Galenica's minority were €5.1m in FY25.
  • Convertible coupons are €5.25m a year in cash; the 110% redemption premium is paid only in 2032.

Judgement for the model.

  • From FY27, free cash flow should run at about 0.9-1.1x net income, with working capital absorbing about 3-5% of incremental revenue.
  • The free cash flow break-even in FY27 is credible. It is "critical to reclaiming long-term investor confidence" (Jefferies).
  • Upside if the inventory buffer unwinds (€10m). Downside if wholesaler terms shorten.
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

F5010th of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Margins are low in level, structurally capped by Rx mix, and rising at the operating line for the first time in years. Gross margin will keep drifting down in percentage terms as Rx mix rises. EBITDA margin is rising because operating costs fall faster than gross margin.

Gross margin.

  • Company adjusted basis: 23.0% (FY24), 22.6% (FY25), 21.5% (H1 2026), 22.1% (Q2 2026, against 23.8% in Q2 2025).
  • FactSet basis: 21.3% (FY23), 20.1% (FY24), 20.2% (FY25), 20.1% (Q2 2026).
  • The 2023 MediService consolidation took it from 28.2% to 25.2%.

Mix arithmetic. Rx earns about 18-22%; non-Rx about 30% (Deutsche Bank). Each percentage point of revenue moving from non-Rx to Rx costs roughly 8-12bp of group gross margin. German Rx rises from about 17% of revenue (FY25) to about 22% (FY26) and plausibly about 25-28% by FY28.

Expect a further fall of about 50-100bp in percentage gross margin by FY28. This is partly offset by:

  • the Fixum (+€0.65 in July 2026, +€0.50 in January 2027, per pack);
  • retail media;
  • own brands;
  • AI pricing.

Consensus gross profit (€746m, €824m, €909m for FY26-28, from five brokers) implies about 21.6%, 21.2% and 21.1% on the company basis. That is consistent with this view.

EBITDA margin (adjusted).

Period Margin
FY22 -0.3%
FY23 3.0%
FY24 1.4%
FY25 2.0% (Q4 2025 about 1.6%)
H1 2026 2.6%
Q2 and Q3 2026 about 3.5% each
FY26 guidance 2.5-3.0%
Consensus FY26 / FY27 / FY28 / FY29 2.9% / 3.5% / 4.2% / 5.2%
Mid-term target 5% (by FY28)
Long-term ambition above 8%

EBIT margin (FactSet adjusted). Negative from FY19 to FY25 (FY25 -0.8%). Consensus 0.6% (FY26), 1.4% (FY27), 2.2% (FY28).

D&A is about 2.0-2.4% of revenue and will rise with Sevenum's depreciation (roughly €8-10m a year on €100m; my estimate). So Sevenum's +50bp at EBITDA is perhaps +20-30bp at EBIT.

Peers:

The structural difference against Apotea is Swiss specialty Rx (very low margin) and German price competition in OTC.

Trend judgement. The EBITDA margin is now on a credible path to 4-4.5% by FY28. 5% needs retail media and marketplace scale, and restraint in co-payment competition.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

D456th of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The earnings base is moderately clean at EBITDA and noisy below it. The adjusted-to-reported gap is material. Several non-obvious leaks sit between EBITDA and EPS, and the Consumer must model them explicitly.

The gap. FY25 adjusted EBITDA (brokers' basis) was €57.4m, against FactSet operating EBITDA of €45.6m: +26%. The adjustments are:

  • ESOP share-based compensation (€1.4m in H1 2026). It is recurring and should be treated as a cost.
  • One-off project costs (for example €1.9m for the nu3 centralisation in FY25).
  • Other items.

Reported net income was negative every year (FY25 -€37.7m). Reported EPS is -€1.87.

Items that flatter or distort:

  1. Capitalised development. Restricted legal reserves under Dutch law signal material capitalisation. It lifts EBITDA and shows up as amortisation inside D&A of about €70m.
  2. Loyalty points revenue deferral. Conservative, not flattering.
  3. Inventory write-downs for expiry. Normal.
  4. Delayed retail media revenue normalised in Q2 2026, helping the gross margin by about 110bp quarter on quarter. Watch for timing effects.

Below-EBITDA leakage, which is what matters for EPS:

  1. Net finance cost. FY25 was €20.3m, inflated by convertible repurchase costs. Consensus implies roughly €25m in FY27 and €31m in FY28 between EBIT and pre-tax profit. The €300m 2032 convertible carries a 1.75% cash coupon (€5.25m) but, under IFRS, a higher effective interest charge, including the 110% redemption premium and the equity component unwinding. Add lease interest on €77m.
  2. Tax. The effective tax rate was 41.2% in H1 2026 (deferred tax on convertible repurchases). FY25 was 17.3%, FY24 3.1%. Consensus seems to assume a low tax charge in FY27-28 (pre-tax €28m to net income €22m; €65m to €56m), presumably using loss carryforwards. Their size is undisclosed in the files: an important unknown.
  3. Minorities. Galenica owns 49% of MediService. That joint venture's earnings count fully in EBITDA, but 49% of its net profit leaks to minorities. MediService's EBITDA is low-margin (about 3% by implication), so the leak is perhaps €3-5m a year (my estimate).
  4. Dilution. 20.1m basic against 20.6m diluted shares. Option cap of 130,000 for the managing board. The convertible could add shares if converted; the conversion price is not in the files.

Net judgement. EBITDA is a fair measure of operating performance once ESOP is added back as a cost. EPS will be noisy and lower than the EBITDA story implies for FY27-28. The broker dispersion (FY27 EPS €0.84 to €2.04) mostly reflects these items.

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

D607th of 11

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Financial leverage is modest in solvency terms and comfortable in maturity terms. In EPS terms it is material, because a largely fixed finance charge (about €25-30m) sits under a thin EBIT.

Debt structure:

  • €300m convertible, 1.75% coupon, redeemed at 110%, due April 2032;
  • €2.6m remaining of the 2021/2028 convertible;
  • €13m bank loan secured on Sevenum automation;
  • leases of €77.4m.

Cash was €119m at June 2026, down from €316m in June 2025 after the convertible redemptions. FactSet net debt is €236m. FactSet net debt/EBITDA is 3.6x trailing on operating EBITDA. On the company's definition it is 1.1x (Q2 2026). No significant maturities before 2030.

Amplification. On consensus FY27, EBIT of €53m less about €25m of finance cost gives pre-tax profit of €28m. A 10% change in EBIT (€5m) therefore moves pre-tax profit by about 19%: financial leverage of about 1.9x on top of the operating leverage. By FY28 (EBIT €96m, finance about €31m) the amplification falls to about 1.5x. In practice, as EBIT grows the gearing effect fades quickly. It is a feature of the inflection years, not a lasting risk.

Comfort.

  • High on liquidity and refinancing. Long-dated, low cash coupon, no covenant issues reported, free cash flow break-even in FY27.
  • The risks: 1. Cash falling further in FY26 (consensus free cash flow -€35m). 2. Dilution, if the convertible converts as the shares rise. 3. Higher effective interest from IFRS accretion. Management repurchased the 2028 converts at an elevated finance expense in 2026.
  • The Risks report's peer precedent (DocMorris' raises) is not relevant at Redcare's scale and funding.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)
